</div></section></section><section aria-label="Article content"><section class="body main-article-body"><section class="abstract" id="abstract1"><h2>Abstract</h2>
<p>We investigated <em>Lepiota</em> species from Benin and reference species from Europe based on fieldwork, light microscopy, and the analysis of DNA sequence data. As a result, thirteen <em>Lepiota</em> species from Benin are described as new to science, whereas only one previously described species, <em>L. albogranulosa</em>, was found. An identification key to these species is provided. The taxonomical results are supported by a comprehensive multilocus phylogeny based on ITS, LSU, and <em>RPB2</em> sequences. In addition, the occurrence of amatoxins in basidiomata of <em>Lepiota</em> spp. was examined, in order to detect toxic mushrooms and for chemotaxonomic conclusions for the genus <em>Lepiota</em> in general. To this end, an UHPLC-HRMS<sup>2</sup> analysis process was applied and molecular networks were generated to obtain an overview of the chemical diversity encompassed by 46 <em>Lepiota</em> species, with particular emphasis on their amatoxin content, resulting in the first report of a <em>Lepiota</em> species containing amatoxin for Benin. The annotation coverage was extended using DEREPLICATOR and taxonomically informed metabolite annotation (TIMA). Birnbaumins were discovered in several species as well as other chemical features of possible chemotaxonomic interest. This study extends the knowledge about diversity, amatoxin occurrence, and possible chemotaxonomic markers in the genus <em>Lepiota</em>.</p>
<p><strong>Citation:</strong> Sarawi S, Reschke K, Jagora A, Yorou NS, Le Pogam P, Piepenbring M (2025). Hidden gems of Benin: Unravelling the diversity of <em>Lepiota</em> spp. through phylogenetic, morphological, and chemotaxonomic exploration. <em>Persoonia</em>
<strong>55</strong>: 313–360. doi: <a href="https://doi.org/10.3114/persoonia.2025.55.09" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">10.3114/persoonia.2025.55.09</a></p>
<section id="kwd-group1" class="kwd-group"><p><strong>Keywords:</strong> <em>Agaricales</em>, amatoxins, new taxa, phallotoxins, phylogeny, taxonomy, HPLC-MS/MS, FBMN, TIMA</p></section></section><section id="s1"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">INTRODUCTION</h2>
<p><em>Lepiota</em> (<em>Agaricaceae</em>) is a worldwide distributed genus which currently is one of the ten largest genera in <em>Agaricales</em> (<a href="#R48" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R48">Kalichman <em>et al.</em> 2020</a>). Many more species remain to be discovered, especially in the tropics (<a href="#R13" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R13">Berkeley &amp; Broome 1871</a>, <a href="#R11" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R11">Beeli 1932</a>, <a href="#R12" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R12">1936</a>, <a href="#R25" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R25">Dennis 1952</a>, <a href="#R26" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R26">1970</a>, <a href="#R75" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R75">Pegler 1972</a>, <a href="#R107" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R107">Vellinga 2004</a>). Recently, several <em>Lepiota</em> species have been newly described from the tropics and subtropics of South America and Southeast Asia (<a href="#R9" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R9">Bashir <em>et al.</em> 2020</a>, <a href="#R43" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R43">Hou &amp; Ge 2020</a>, <a href="#R100" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R100">Sysouphanthong <em>et al.</em> 2020</a>, <a href="#R5" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R5">Asif <em>et al.</em> 2022</a>, <a href="#R34" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R34">Haqnawaz <em>et al.</em> 2022</a>, <a href="#R1" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R1">Ahamed <em>et al.</em> 2023</a>, <a href="#R63" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R63">Liang <em>et al.</em> 2023</a>, <a href="#R65" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R65">Mao <em>et al.</em> 2023</a>, <a href="#R84" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R84">Roy <em>et al.</em> 2023</a>, <a href="#R6" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R6">Asif <em>et al.</em> 2024</a>, <a href="#R7" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R7">Azeem <em>et al.</em> 2024</a>, <a href="#R82" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R82">Rehman <em>et al.</em> 2024</a>). Despite the early work of Beeli and Heinemann in the Democratic Republic of Congo and Central Africa (<a href="#R11" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R11">Beeli 1932</a>, <a href="#R12" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R12">1936</a>, <a href="#R35" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R35">Heinemann 1973</a>, <a href="#R36" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R36">1977</a>, <a href="#R37" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R37">1980</a>), many <em>Lepiota</em> species in Africa remain undiscovered and undescribed. For West Africa, 18 species of <em>Lepiota</em> have been recorded, but not even one from Benin (<a href="#R78" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R78">Piepenbring <em>et al.</em> 2020</a>). West Africa provides suitable habitats for a variety of fungal species due to its diverse ecosystems, ranging from tropical rainforests to savannas. However, mycological studies in the region remain limited, and Benin in particular, has been largely overlooked in fungal biodiversity research.</p>
<p><em>Lepiota</em> species are characterized by basidiomata with universal and partial veils, free lamellae, whitish spore powder, predominantly dextrinoid basidiospores, and clamp connections (<a href="#R106" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R106">Vellinga 2003</a>). According to <a href="#R90" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R90">Sarawi <em>et al.</em> (2025)</a>, the genus <em>Lepiota</em> can be divided into seven sections based on pileus covering and basidiospore shapes: section <em>Stenosporae</em>, with a trichoderm and spurred basidiospores; section <em>Helveolae</em>, with a trichoderm and ovoid to ellipsoid basidiospores; section <em>Christatae</em>, with a hymeniderm or a pileus covering consisting of (sub)globose cells arranged in chains, ovoid to ellipsoid or spurred basidiospores, and brownish pileus colours; section <em>Lilaceae</em>, with a hymeniderm pileus covering, ovoid to ellipsoid basidiospores, and violet or vivid orange-brown pileus colours; section <em>Lepiota</em>, with a trichodermal pileus covering and fusiform to penguin-shaped basidiospores; section <em>Fuscovinaceae</em>, with a cutis and ovoid to ellipsoid basidiospores; and section <em>Eriophorae</em>, with a pileus covering consisting of (sub)globose cells arranged in chains and ovoid to ellipsoid basidiospores. The phylogenetic positions of these sections and the species they include were examined using ITS and LSU, a low number of IGS sequences, <em>RPB2</em>, or mtSSU sequence data, and correlated with morphological character states (<a href="#R106" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R106">Vellinga 2003</a>, <a href="#R62" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R62">Liang <em>et al.</em> 2018</a>, <a href="#R43" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R43">Hou &amp; Ge 2020</a>, <a href="#R90" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R90">Sarawi <em>et al.</em> 2025</a>).</p>
<p>Certain <em>Lepiota</em> species are of medical concern due to the presence of amatoxins in their basidiomata. Amatoxins are bicyclic octapeptides that cause severe liver damage and even death by inhibiting RNA polymerase II (<a href="#R118" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R118">Wieland <em>et al.</em> 1981</a>). They are responsible for 95 % of all fatal mushroom poisonings worldwide (<a href="#R33" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R33">Gicquel <em>et al.</em> 2014</a>). Amatoxins also occur in two other, not closely related genera, viz., <em>Amanita</em> (<em>Amanitaceae</em>) and <em>Galerina</em> (<em>Strophariaceae</em>) (<a href="#R64" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R64">Luo <em>et al.</em> 2018</a>). The distribution of amatoxins within <em>Lepiota</em> has long been unclear; however, a recent study demonstrated that fewer <em>Lepiota</em> species contain amatoxins than previously assumed and that they are apparently restricted to subsection <em>Helveolinae</em> (<a href="#R89" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R89">Sarawi <em>et al.</em> 2022</a>).</p>
<p>In addition to amatoxins, another group of cyclic toxins, namely phallotoxins, exists. These bicyclic heptapeptides interact with the F-actin filaments and thus affect the cell structure by inhibiting the depolymerisation of F-actin (<a href="#R117" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R117">Wieland 1977</a>). It has been demonstrated that phallotoxins are not absorbed via the gastrointestinal tract and are therefore not responsible for the toxicity of certain species (<a href="#R119" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R119">Wieland 1983</a>). So far, phallotoxins have not been reported for <em>Lepiota</em> species (<a href="#R93" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R93">Sgambelluri <em>et al.</em> 2014</a>, <a href="#R89" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R89">Sarawi <em>et al.</em> 2022</a>).</p>
<p>So far, only one study is known concerning molecular network analysis for the genus <em>Lepiota</em>, in which <em>L. brunneoincarnata</em> and species belonging to the genus <em>Amanita</em> were analysed for amatoxins and phallotoxins; however, this study does not consider chemotaxonomy (<a href="#R123" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R123">Zhao <em>et al.</em> 2023</a>). Expanding on this approach, Feature Based Molecular Networking enables the mapping of large quantities of tandem mass spectrometry data, such as those obtained by (U)HPLC-HRMS<sup>2</sup> of numerous samples. Compounds with similar fragmentation patterns cluster, and therefore probably correspond to structurally related structures (<a href="#R121" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R121">Yang <em>et al.</em> 2013</a>). In the process, these tandem mass spectrometry data are also compared to reference MS/MS spectra held in the GNPS spectral repositories to annotate nodes that are analogous to spectroscopic data deposited in these libraries (<a href="#R115" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R115">Wang <em>et al.</em> 2016</a>, <a href="#R73" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R73">Nothias <em>et al.</em> 2020</a>). Molecular networks can be enriched with functional information relevant to a given study (e.g. bioactivity, phylogenetic information) to identify compounds with specific properties of interest in a given study and, potentially, to streamline subsequent isolation for full structural elucidation. Innovative chemoinformatic tools are continuously being developed to extend the annotation coverage to compounds for which experimental MS/MS data have not been uploaded yet to the GNPS spectral libraries such as DEREPLICATOR, a dereplication algorithm dedicated to the identification of peptide natural products from their tandem mass spectra (<a href="#R68" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R68">Mohimani <em>et al.</em> 2017</a>). Recorded MS/MS spectra can also be compared against in silico MS/MS fragmentation database (ISDB), through tools like the TIMA v. 2.9.3 package and LOTUS database (<a href="#R2" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R2">Allard <em>et al.</em> 2016</a>, <a href="#R85" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R85">Rutz <em>et al.</em> 2019</a>, <a href="#R86" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R86">Rutz <em>et al.</em> 2022</a>). In short, this latter strategy considers not only the spectral similarity score, but also the taxonomic distance between the biological source of the annotated compound and the biological source of the candidate structures, as well as the structural consistency between the different nodes of a group (structural consistency being expected within the different nodes of a group) (<a href="#R85" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R85">Rutz <em>et al.</em> 2019</a>).</p>
<p>By integrating these techniques, molecular networking provides valuable insights into the chemical diversity of <em>Lepiota</em> species, facilitating the discovery of novel bioactive compounds and improving our understanding of fungal secondary metabolism.</p></section><section id="s2"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">MATERIALS AND METHODS</h2>
<section id="s2_1"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Fieldwork and morphological studies</h3>
<p><em>Lepiota</em> specimens were collected during mycological surveys in June and July 2022 at different sites in Benin, West Africa. They were found in gallery forests, woodlands, and savannahs with trees identified as <em>Berlinia grandiflora</em>, <em>Isoberlinia doka</em>, <em>I. tomentosa</em>, <em>Monotes kerstingii</em>, <em>Uapaca guineensis</em>, and/or <em>Uapaca togoensis</em>, among other species. The macroscopic characteristics of the freshly collected basidiomata were recorded in the field. Colour codes were assigned using <a href="#R54" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R54">Kornerup &amp; Wanscher (1978)</a>. The basidiomata were air-dried on an electric food dehydrator at 40 °C. The microscopic features were examined using light microscopes. Microscopic characteristics of the dried material were studied in water or 5–10 % KOH. Some sections were stained with ammoniacal 10 % Congo Red. Melzer’s reagent was used to study the dextrinoidity and Cresyl blue (0.1 % in H<sub>2</sub>O) for metachromasy of the basidiospores. Measurements are given, with average value and extreme values in brackets. The basidiospore measurements are presented along with the information (n/m/p), denoting ‘n’ basidiospores from ‘m’ basidiomata of ‘p’ collections. The specimens from Benin are deposited in the Herbarium FR, Frankfurt am Main, Germany, and isotypes are deposited in the mycological herbarium of the University of Parakou, UNIPAR, Benin.</p></section><section id="s2_2"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">DNA extraction, PCR amplification and sequencing</h3>
<p>For DNA extraction, small pieces of fresh basidiomata were stored in double cetyl-trimethylammonium bromide (CTAB). These pieces were later frozen with liquid nitrogen and ground to a fine powder using an MM301 Mixer Mill (Retsch GmbH, Germany). The extraction was performed using the innuPREP plant DNA kit (IST Innuscreen, Germany) following the manufacturer’s instructions. The PCR was performed using VWR <em>Taq</em> DNA polymerase (VWR, Germany) in a peqStar gradient thermal cycler (PEQLAB, Germany). For the amplification of the nuclear ribosomal internal transcribed spacer (ITS) region, the primer pairs ITS1F or ITS5 together with ITS4B or ITS4 (<a href="#R116" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R116">White <em>et al.</em> 1990</a>, <a href="#R30" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R30">Gardes &amp; Bruns 1993</a>) and for the amplification of the 28S nuclear ribosomal large subunit region (LSU), the primer pair LR0R/LR7 were used (<a href="#R112" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R112">Vilgalys &amp; Hester 1990</a>, <a href="#R23" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R23">Cubeta <em>et al.</em> 1991</a>). For the protein encoding gene region RNA polymerase II second largest subunit (<em>RPB2</em>), the primer pair bRPB2-6F/rpb2-7.1R (<a href="#R66" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R66">Matheny 2005</a>) was used. The PCR products were verified by gel electrophoresis using a 1 % (w/v) agarose gel and purified using the QIAquick PCR purification kit (QIAGEN, Germany). The purified PCR products were sequenced by Sanger sequencing at Microsynth SeqLab (Germany). All sequences generated are deposited in GenBank (<a href="#T1" class="usa-link">Table 1</a>).</p>

<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="table/T1/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div></section></section><section id="s2_3"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Phylogenetic analysis</h3>
<p>The generated DNA sequences were edited, assembled and quality checked using Geneious Prime v. 2021.2.2 (Biomatters Ltd., New Zealand). Additional sequences from a previous study (<a href="#R89" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R89">Sarawi <em>et al.</em> 2022</a>) supplemented with sequences from GenBank were used for the phylogenetic analysis (<a href="#T1" class="usa-link">Table 1</a>). To ensure comprehensive taxonomic and geographic representation, we selected sequences of all major sections of the genus <em>Lepiota</em>, with a particular emphasis on multilocus data. Furthermore, we included sequences from geographically diverse populations, covering both temperate and tropical regions, while retaining predominantly those sequences with high similarity (≥ 90 % identity) to our samples. The final single-gene datasets were then aligned separately in MAFFT v. 7 (<a href="#R51" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R51">Katoh &amp; Standley 2013</a>) using the E-INS-i algorithm for the ITS dataset and the FFT-NS-i algorithm for the LSU and <em>RPB2</em> datasets (<a href="#R50" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R50">Katoh &amp; Toh 2008</a>). The alignments were checked and pruned in MEGA v. 7 (<a href="#R55" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R55">Kumar <em>et al.</em> 2016</a>). A Maximum-Likelihood analysis was conducted for each dataset using RAxML v. 8.2.12 (<a href="#R96" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R96">Stamatakis 2014</a>) applying the GTRCAT model with 25 per site rate categories and 1000 bootstrap iterations (<a href="#R29" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R29">Felsenstein 1985</a>). Since no major topological inconsistencies were found between the Maximum-Likelihood phylogenies of the individual gene loci, the alignments were concatenated and a Maximum-Likelihood analysis was performed as described above using a partitioned model with the three partitions, ITS, LSU, and <em>RPB2</em>. Transfer bootstrap expectations (TBE) were obtained for this analysis via BoosterWeb (<a href="#R58" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R58">Lemoine <em>et al.</em> 2018</a>). A Bayesian Markov Chain Monte Carlo (MCMC) analysis was performed using MrBayes v. 3.2.7a on XSEDE (<a href="#R44" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R44">Huelsenbeck &amp; Ronquist 2001</a>, <a href="#R83" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R83">Ronquist <em>et al.</em> 2012</a>), with two runs of 10 M generations with four chains each, a sampling frequency every 1000 generations, and a stoprule for topological convergence diagnostics falling below 0.01. The GTR model and a gamma distribution was used. The first 25 % generations were discarded as burn-in. The Maximum-Likelihood and Bayesian Markov Chain Monte Carlo analyses were performed via the Cipres Science Gateway (<a href="#R67" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R67">Miller <em>et al.</em> 2010</a>). Phylogenetic trees were visualized using TreeGraph2 (<a href="#R97" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R97">Stöver &amp; Müller 2010</a>).</p>
<p>The alignments and trees are deposited in Figshare (<a href="https://doi.org/10.6084/m9.figshare.25827439" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">https://doi.org/10.6084/m9.figshare.25827439</a>).</p></section><section id="s2_4"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Natural compound extraction and instrument conditions</h3>
<p>For the extraction of natural compounds, dry material of each specimen was frozen in liquid nitrogen and subsequently grounded to a fine powder using an MM301 Mixer Mill (Retsch GmbH, Germany). The powder was then suspended in a methanol:dichloromethane (1:1; <em>v</em>:<em>v</em>) mixture, shaken and incubated at room temperature overnight. Supernatants were obtained by centrifugation at 4000 × g for 10 min and freeze dried using an RVC 2-33 CDplus Rotational vacuum concentrator with a CT 04-50 cold trap (both from Martin Christ Gefriertrocknungsanlagen GmbH, Germany). The dried extracts were suspended in methanol to a final concentration of 2 mg/mL.</p>
<p>The LC-ESI-HRMS<sup>2</sup> analyses were performed on an Agilent 1290 Infinity II UHPLC coupled to a hybrid quadrupole time of flight (QTOF) mass spectrometer Agilent 6546 (Agilent Technologies) equipped with an ESI source, operating in positive ion mode. A BEH Waters Acquity C<sub>18</sub> UPLC column (2.1 × 100 mm; 1.7 µm) was used, with water + 0.1 % formic acid (A) and acetonitrile + 0.1 % formic acid (B) being used as mobile phase. The flow rate was set to 0.5 mL/min. A linear gradient from 5 % to 100 % B in 12 min followed by 100 % B for 4 min was used. Source parameters were set as follows: capillary temperature at 320 °C, source voltage at 3500 V, sheath gas flow rate at 11 L/min. The MS scans were operated in full-scan mode from <em>m/z</em> 100 to 1200 (0.1 s scan time) with a mass resolution of 60.000 at <em>m/z</em> = 922. An MS1 scan was followed by MS2 scans of the three most intense ions above an absolute threshold of 3,000 counts. Selected parent ions were fragmented at a collision energy fixed at 25 eV and an isolation window of 1.3 <em>m/z</em>. The purine (M+H)<sup>+</sup> ion (C<sub>5</sub>H<sub>5</sub>N<sub>4</sub><sup>+</sup>, <em>m/z</em> = 121.0509) and hexakis (<sup>1</sup>H, <sup>1</sup>H, <sup>3</sup>H-tetrafluoropropoxy)phosphazene (M+H)<sup>+</sup> ion (C<sub>18</sub>H<sub>19</sub>F<sub>24</sub>N<sub>3</sub>O<sub>6</sub>P<sub>3</sub><sup>+</sup>, <em>m/z</em> = 922.0098) were used as internal lock masses and a permanent MS/MS exclusion list criterion was set to prevent oversampling of the internal calibrant. The LC-UV and MS/MS data acquisition and processing were performed using MassHunter Workstation software (Agilent Technologies).</p></section><section id="s2_5"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">MS/MS data preprocessing</h3>
<p>The MS data were converted from the Agilent standard .d format to .mzXL format using MSConvert, part of the ProteoWizard package (<a href="#R21" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R21">Chambers <em>et al.</em> 2012</a>). The .mzXL files were processed using MZMine 3 v. 3.9.0 (<a href="#R91" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R91">Schmid <em>et al.</em> 2023</a>). The threshold for mass detection was set to 1.5E3 for the MS<sup>1</sup> and 7.0E2 for the MS<sup>2</sup>. The ADAP chromatogram builder algorithm was used with a minimum group size of scans of three, a group intensity threshold of 2.0E3, a minimum height intensity of 3.0E4, and a <em>m/z</em> tolerance of 0.005 Da or 10 ppm. For chromatogram deconvolution, the Local minimum resolver algorithm was selected with a chromatographic threshold of 90 %, a minimum search range in retention time of 0.05 min, a minimum relative height of 0 %, a minimum absolute height of 3E4, a minimum ratio of peak top/edge of two, a peak duration range of 0–1 min, and a minimum of three data points. For pairing the MS<sup>2</sup> scans, a <em>m/z</em> tolerance of 0.02 Da or 10 ppm and a retention time tolerance of 0.15 min were set. Isotope grouping was performed using the <sup>13</sup>C isotope filter algorithm, with the most intense peak selected as the representative isotope, having a <em>m/z</em> tolerance of 0.001 or 3 ppm and a retention time tolerance of 0.05 min. The peak alignment was performed using the join aligner, with an <em>m/z</em> tolerance of 0.005 Da or 10 ppm, a retention time tolerance of 0.2 min, and equivalent weights for both. The feature list row filter was used to keep only datasets containing MS<sup>2</sup> scans. Gap filling was performed with an intensity tolerance of 20 %, an <em>m/z</em> tolerance of 0.002 or 10 ppm, a retention time tolerance of 0.05 min. The “Export/Submit for GNPS/FBMN” option was used to export the processed data in .mgf and .csv format.</p></section><section id="s2_6"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Molecular Network Generation and DEREPLICATOR annotation</h3>
<p>A feature-based molecular network (FBMN) analysis was performed with the exported files using the FBMN online workflow v_28.2 via GNPS (<a href="#R115" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R115">Wang <em>et al.</em> 2016</a>, <a href="#R73" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R73">Nothias <em>et al.</em> 2020</a>) with a precursor ion mass tolerance of 0.02 Da and a fragment ion tolerance of 0.02 Da. The molecular network was created where the edges were filtered to have a cosine score above 0.6 and more than five matched peaks. Edges between two nodes were kept only if each of the nodes appeared in the top 10 most similar nodes of the connected feature (network TopK = 10). Matches against the spectral libraries of the GNPS required a cosine score above 0.7 and at least 5 matched peaks. Cytoscape v. 3.10.2 was used to visualize and analyse the molecular networking data (<a href="#R94" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R94">Shannon <em>et al.</em> 2003</a>). The GNPS job parameters and resulting data are available at the following address <a href="https://gnps.ucsd.edu/ProteoSAFe/status.jsp?task=e527f0adec444f6c9f639781e6616884" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">https://gnps.ucsd.edu/ProteoSAFe/status.jsp?task=e527f0adec444f6c9f639781e6616884</a> and the data resulting from the DEREPLICATOR job (variable dereplication process) are available at <a href="https://gnps.ucsd.edu/ProteoSAFe/status.jsp?task=71fc9eb966da4de49c2c97c9038f5d09" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">https://gnps.ucsd.edu/ProteoSAFe/status.jsp?task=71fc9eb966da4de49c2c97c9038f5d09</a>. Since the general molecular network could not discriminate between amaninamide (<em>m/z</em> = 903.3663, tR = 2.84 min) and γ-amanitin (<em>m/z</em> = 903.3661, tR = 2.67 min), a second FBMN workflow was undertaken to address this issue. These toxins could indeed be discriminated using identical processing parameters except for the retention time tolerance of the join aligner module where a more stringent value of 0.1 min was retained. This additional molecular network is made available at <a href="https://gnps.ucsd.edu/ProteoSAFe/status.jsp?task=34abc1cb556f4a4d9b0359bab66502c0" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">https://gnps.ucsd.edu/ProteoSAFe/status.jsp?task=34abc1cb556f4a4d9b0359bab66502c0</a> and the data resulting from the DEREPLICATOR job (variable dereplication process) are available at <a href="https://gnps.ucsd.edu/ProteoSAFe/status.jsp?task=80a5cd357c0d4401bc6f95dec578cb25" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">https://gnps.ucsd.edu/ProteoSAFe/status.jsp?task=80a5cd357c0d4401bc6f95dec578cb25</a>.</p></section><section id="s2_7"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">ISDB annotation and taxonomically informed reconsideration</h3>
<p>The taxonomically informed metabolite annotations were made using TIMA, following the documentation in this repository (v. 2.9.3) and re-ranking from the taxonomical information available on LOTUS (<a href="#R2" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R2">Allard <em>et al.</em> 2016</a>, <a href="#R85" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R85">Rutz <em>et al.</em> 2019</a>, <a href="#R86" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R86">Rutz <em>et al.</em> 2022</a>, <a href="#R87" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R87">Rutz <em>et al.</em> 2024</a>). The ISDB used for this process includes the combined records of the Dictionary of Natural Products (v. 32.2) and the LOTUS Initiative records (v. 4.1) (<a href="#R3" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R3">Allard <em>et al.</em> 2023</a>). For the TIMA annotation-related parameters the number of final candidates was set to three, the minimal chemical consistency score to consider a class and the minimal similarity score were set to 0, while the minimal biological score and minimal chemical score to keep MS1 only annotation were set to 0.6. The weight for the biological information was set to 0.5, the weight for the chemical information was set to 0.166, and the weight for the spectroscopic matching was set to 0.333 (<a href="#R38" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R38">Hell <em>et al.</em> 2022</a>). The biological score matches were set as follows: domain 0.1, kingdom 0.2, phylum 0.3, class 0.4, order 0.5, family 0.6, tribe 0.7, genus 0.8, subgenus 0.85, species 0.9, and variety 1. The chemical score matches were set as follows: kingdom 0.25, superclass 0.5, class 0.75, and parent 1. The option parameter “Summarise results to one row per feature” was chosen.</p></section></section><section id="s3"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">RESULTS</h2>
<section id="s3_1"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Phylogenetic analysis</h3>
<p>For this study, a total of 353 sequences of 60 specimens of <em>Lepiota</em> species recently collected in Benin and of 72 specimens of <em>Lepiota</em> recently collected in Europe were generated. They comprise 108 ITS sequences, 125 LSU sequences, and 119 <em>RPB2</em> sequences representing all sections in <em>Lepiota</em>. Single-locus trees derived from the sequence data did not exhibit conflicting phylogenies (Supplementary <a href="#per-2025-55-9_SF1" class="usa-link">Figs S1</a>–<a href="#per-2025-55-9_SF3" class="usa-link">S3</a>). However, the support values for the LSU single-locus tree were low compared to those of the other gene loci. Multilocus phylogenies, using concatenated alignments of ITS and <em>RPB2</em>, as well as ITS, LSU, and <em>RPB2</em> sequences, were constructed from the data (Supplementary <a href="#per-2025-55-9_SF4" class="usa-link">Fig. S4</a>). Once again, the tree topology and branch support were consistent, with no topological incongruence between Maximum-Likelihood and Bayesian analyses. The multilocus phylogeny delineated several clades, corresponding to the sections within the genus <em>Lepiota</em>, namely <em>Christatae</em>, <em>Eriophorae</em>, <em>Fuscovinaceae</em>, <em>Lepiota</em>, <em>Lilaceae</em>, <em>Helveolae</em>, and <em>Stenosporae</em>. However, the precise positions of the individual sections related to each other were not well supported. The positions of the sequences from specimens from Benin are well supported and they cluster among sequences obtained from specimens from various other geographical regions, without forming distinct clades.</p></section><section id="s3_2"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Taxonomy</h3>
<p>The morphological and molecular phylogenetic results revealed the recently described species <em>L. albogranulosa</em>, known from Pakistan and thirteen hitherto undescribed <em>Lepiota</em> species, belonging to the sections <em>Eriophorae</em>, <em>Helveolae</em>, <em>Lepiota</em>, <em>Lilaceae</em>, and <em>Stenosporae</em>.</p>
<section id="s3_2_1"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">Section <em>Eriophorae</em>
</h4>
<p><strong><em>Lepiota echinobispora</em></strong> Sarawi &amp; Reschke, <strong><em>sp. nov.</em></strong> MB 854014. <a href="#F2" class="usa-link">Fig. 2</a>.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="F2"><h5 class="obj_head">Fig. 2.</h5>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12798316_per-2025-55-9_F2.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/d006/12798316/098cf007a535/per-2025-55-9_F2.jpg" loading="lazy" height="969" width="704" alt="Fig. 2"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/F2/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Basidiomata and microstructures of <em>Lepiota echinobispora</em>. <strong>A.</strong> Basidioma (SeSa115, holotype). <strong>B.</strong> Basidioma (SeSa114). <strong>C.</strong> Basidioma (SeSa345), seen from above (left) and from below the pileus (right). <strong>D.</strong> Basidiospores (SeSa115). <strong>E.</strong> Basidia (SeSa115). <strong>F.</strong> Cheilocystidia (SeSa115). <strong>G.</strong> Pileus covering (SeSa115). Scale bar: A–C = 1 cm; D = 3 µm; E, F = 10 µm; G = 20 µm.</p></figcaption></figure><p><em>Etymology</em>: <em>echinos</em> (Greek) = spiny, <em>bi</em> (Latin) = two, <em>spora</em> (Greek) = spore; refers to the echinate pileus covering this species possesses and the predominantly bispored basidia.</p>
<p><em>Typus</em>: <strong>Benin</strong>, Borgou Department, Okpara, Forêt de Okpara N9.237, E2.724, alt. 200 m.a.s.l., on sandy soil, in woodland dominated by <em>Isoberlinia doka</em> and <em>Uapaca togoensis</em>, 12 Jun. 2022, <em>S. Sarawi, S. Badou, D. Dongnima, F. Hampe, C. Manz, B. Olou &amp; A. Rühl</em>, SeSa115 (<strong>holotype</strong> FR-0175201, <strong>isotype</strong> in UNIPAR). GenBank: ITS = <a href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/nuccore/PP594560" class="usa-link" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">PP594560</a>, LSU = <a href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/nuccore/PP594678" class="usa-link" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">PP594678</a>, <em>RPB2</em> = <a href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/nuccore/PP841184" class="usa-link" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">PP841184</a>.</p>
<p><em>Basidiomata</em> small to medium. <em>Pileus</em> 10–25 mm diam., initially hemispherical to conical, becoming convex to plano-convex, occasionally slightly umbonate, with concentrically arranged conical spines with curved tips, spines brown to dark brown or greyish brown (6E4–8, 6F4–8, 7E4–5), densely clustered at the centre, scattered towards margin; centre of the pileus with brown background (6E4–8, 6F4–8), paler, light brown to cream towards margin (6A2–3, 6D4–5). <em>Lamellae</em> free, moderately crowded to crowded, ventricose, 1–3 mm deep, white to cream; edge concolourous; lamellulae in up to two tiers. <em>Stipe</em> 10–35 × 1–3 mm, cylindrical, at apex whitish to cream or light brown (6A2–3, 6D4–5) gradually darker brown or greyish brown (6E4–E8, 6F4–8, 7E4–5) below annular zone, in lower half with indistinct spines that are concolorous with pileus spines. <em>Annulus</em> not observed. <em>Odour</em> and <em>taste</em> not recorded. <em>Basidiospores</em> (120/3/3) (3.5–)3.9–4.2–4.5(–5.6) × (2.4–)2.6–2.9–3.1(–3.8) µm, Q = 1.24–1.87, Q<sub>av</sub> = 1.43–1.53, ovoid to ellipsoid, hyaline, smooth, dextrinoid, non-metachromatic. <em>Basidia</em> (13–)15–19(–23) × (4.5–)5.4–6.6(–7.3) µm, narrowly clavate to clavate, hyaline, with oily content, predominantly 2-spored, but also frequently 4-spored, occasionally 1-spored, with up to 4 µm long sterigmata, when 1- or 2-spored with longer and thicker sterigmata up to 7 µm long. <em>Lamella edge</em> mostly sterile, sometimes heterogeneous with some fertile parts. <em>Cheilocystidia</em> (10–)13–20(–26) × (3.5–)4.6–6.0(–6.9) µm, cylindrical or narrowly clavate to clavate, hyaline. <em>Pleurocystidia</em> absent. <em>Pileus covering</em> composed of two types of agglutinated chains forming spines, i.e., chains formed by spherical cells and less abundant chains formed by cylindrical cells; terminal cells of chains formed by spherical cells, (16–)21–32(–41) µm in diam., (sub)globose; basal cells cylindrical or narrowly clavate to clavate. The other type of chains consists of cylindrical to elongated cells, (21–)28–40(–46) × (7–)8–12(–14) µm; terminal cells with rounded apex. Both type of chains with pale brown to brown parietal pigment. <em>Clamp connections</em> present in all parts.</p>
<p><em>Ecology, habitat, and distribution</em>: Basidiomata solitary, probably saprotrophic, on soil, in woodland. Currently only known from Benin.</p>
<p><em>Toxicity</em>: No amatoxins and phallotoxins were detected.</p>
<p><em>Additional specimens examined</em>: <strong>Benin</strong>, Borgou Department, Okpara, Forêt de Okpara, N9.237, E2.724, alt. 200 m.a.s.l., on sandy soil, in woodland dominated by <em>Isoberlinia doka</em> and <em>Uapaca togoensis</em>, 12 Jun. 2022, <em>S. Sarawi, S. Badou, D. Dongnima, F. Hampe, C. Manz, B. Olou &amp; A. Rühl</em>, SeSa114; Collines Department, between Djalloukou and Konkon, N7.730, E1.839, alt. 190 m.a.s.l., on sandy soil, in woodland with <em>Isoberlinia doka</em>, 3 Jul. 2022, <em>S. Sarawi, D. Dongnima, F. Hampe, C. Manz &amp; A. Rühl</em>, SeSa345.</p>
<p><em>Notes</em>: <em>Lepiota echinobispora</em> in section <em>Eriophorae</em>, is characterized by a light brown, brown to greyish brown coloured pileus with conical spines, dextrinoid, ellipsoid to ovoid basidiospores, predominantly 2-spored basidia, the presence of cheilocystidia, and the cellular structure of the spines, which are composed of chains consisting of either spherical or cylindrical cells. Phylogenetically, <em>L. echinobispora</em> is close to <em>L. echinacea</em>, with a p-distance of 6 % in the ITS sequence. Macroscopically, distinguishing <em>L. echinobispora</em> from <em>L. echinacea</em> can be challenging due to the latter’s variable micromorphology. However, microscopically, <em>L. echinobispora</em> differs from <em>L. echinacea</em> by the presence of cheilocystidia, which are either absent or only present in tufts in <em>L. echinacea</em>, predominantly 2-spored basidia, compared to mostly 4-spored basidia in <em>L. echinacea</em>, and the pileus covering of <em>L. echinacea</em>, which is formed by only one type of chain formed by spherical cells (<a href="#R57" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R57">Lange 1940</a>, <a href="#R53" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R53">Knudsen 1980</a>, <a href="#R103" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R103">Vellinga 2001a</a>).</p>
<p>Another morphologically similar species, though not closely related, is <em>L. jacobi</em>. <em>Lepiota jacobi</em> can be distinguished from <em>L. echinobispora</em> by its darker brown basidiomata, 4-spored basidia, and absence of cheilocystidia (<a href="#R53" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R53">Knudsen 1980</a>, <a href="#R102" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R102">Vellinga 1992</a>, <a href="#R103" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R103">2001a</a>). <em>Lepiota carinii</em> can be differentiated from <em>L. echinobispora</em> by its yellow-brown colours, the distinctly spiny stipe, and 4-spored basidia (<a href="#R18" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R18">Bresadola 1930</a>, <a href="#R53" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R53">Knudsen 1980</a>, <a href="#R103" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R103">Vellinga 2001a</a>).</p>
<p>Several other similar looking <em>Lepiota</em> species are known from older studies in East and Central Africa and other (sub-)tropical regions but these lack molecular sequence data. However, <em>L. kigumuensis</em> described from Kenya is morphologically similar to <em>L. echinobispora</em>. <em>Lepiota kigumuensis</em> differs from <em>L. echinobispora</em> by minute, loose, ochraceous brown squamules and significantly larger basidiospores, 4.7–6.5 × 3.0–4.3 (<a href="#R76" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R76">Pegler 1977</a>).</p>
<p><strong><em>Lepiota minutoflava</em></strong> Sarawi, <strong><em>sp. nov.</em></strong> MB 854015. <a href="#F3" class="usa-link">Fig. 3</a>.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="F3"><h5 class="obj_head">Fig. 3.</h5>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12798316_per-2025-55-9_F3.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/d006/12798316/473edf044cc0/per-2025-55-9_F3.jpg" loading="lazy" height="824" width="700" alt="Fig. 3"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/F3/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Basidiomata and microstructures of <em>Lepiota minutoflava</em> (SeSa239, holotype). <strong>A.</strong> Basidiomata. <strong>B.</strong> Basidiospores. <strong>C.</strong> Basidia. <strong>D.</strong> Cheilocystidia. <strong>E.</strong> Pileus covering. Scale bar: A = 5 mm; B = 3 µm; C, D = 10 µm; E = 20 µm.</p></figcaption></figure><p><em>Etymology</em>: <em>minutus</em> (Latin) = very small, <em>flavus</em> (Latin) = yellow; refers to the yellow colour and the small size of the basidiomata.</p>
<p><em>Typus</em>: <strong>Benin</strong>, Collines Department, Ekpa, N8.601, E2.600, alt. 320 m.a.s.l., on sandy soil, in woodland dominated by <em>Isoberlinia doka</em>, 23 Jun. 2022, <em>S. Sarawi, D. Dongnima, F. Hampe, C. Manz &amp; A. Rühl</em>, SeSa239 (<strong>holotype</strong> FR-0175168, <strong>isotype</strong> in UNIPAR). GenBank: ITS = <a href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/nuccore/PP594576" class="usa-link" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">PP594576</a>, LSU = <a href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/nuccore/PP594696" class="usa-link" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">PP594696</a>, <em>RPB2</em> = <a href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/nuccore/PP841204" class="usa-link" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">PP841204</a>.</p>
<p><em>Basidiomata</em> very small to small. <em>Pileus</em> 2–5 mm diam., initially hemispherical to conical or campanulate, becoming convex to plano-convex, at centre yellow to orange or orange-brown (5A7–8, 5B7–8, C6–8), gradually paler, light orange-brown to pale yellow towards margin (5A5–6, 5B4–6, 5C5–6), covered by minuscule, granulose, brown to orange-brown warts. <em>Lamellae</em> free, moderately crowded to distant, ventricose, 0.5–1.5 mm deep, pale yellow to light orange (4A3–5, 5A4–5); edge concolourous; with one lamellula. <em>Stipe</em> 10–20 × 0.5–1 mm, cylindrical, at apex pale yellow to light orange or light brown (5A2–5, 5B3–5, 5C4–5), gradually darker, orange-brown (5A6–8, 5C5–8, 5D5–6) towards base, with minute orange-brown warts. <em>Annulus</em> not observed. <em>Odour</em> and <em>taste</em> not recorded. <em>Basidiospores</em> (80/2/2) (3.8–)4.3–4.7–5.1(–5.6) × (2.6–)2.8–3.1–3.4(–3.7) µm, Q = 1.26–1.75, Q<sub>av</sub> = 1.50–1.53, ovoid to ellipsoid, hyaline, smooth, dextrinoid, non-metachromatic. <em>Basidia</em> (15–)18–22(–25) × (5.5–)6.0–7.2(–8.0) µm, narrowly clavate to clavate, hyaline, with oily content, yellowish in water and KOH, 4-spored, occasionally 2-spored; sterigmata up to 5 µm long, sometimes relatively long and broad, up to 7 × 2 µm. <em>Lamella edge</em> sterile to heterogenous with abundant cheilocystidia and some basidia in between. <em>Cheilocystidia</em> (14–)19–30(–39) × (5.3–)6.4–10.4(–13.4) µm, cylindrical or narrowly clavate to clavate, hyaline. <em>Pleurocystidia</em> absent. <em>Pileus covering</em> made up of chains with terminal cells measuring (11–)12–24(–34) µm in diam., (sub)globose; basal cells narrowly clavate to clavate, with pale brown parietal pigment. <em>Clamp connections</em> present in all parts.</p>
<p><em>Ecology, habitat, and distribution</em>: Basidiomata gregarious in small groups, probably saprotrophic, on soil, in woodland and savannah. Currently only known from Benin.</p>
<p><em>Toxicity</em>: No amatoxins and phallotoxins were detected.</p>
<p><em>Additional specimen examined</em>: <strong>Benin</strong>, Atacora Department, Kota waterfalls, N10.212, E1.444, alt. 500 m.a.s.l., on sandy soil, in savannah with <em>Berlinia grandiflora</em>, <em>Isoberlinia tomentosa</em>, and <em>Uapaca guinensis</em>, 26 Jun. 2022, <em>S. Sarawi, D. Dongnima, F. Hampe, C. Manz &amp; A. Rühl</em>, SeSa283.</p>
<p><em>Notes</em>: <em>Lepiota minutoflava</em>, section <em>Eriophorae</em>, is characterized by minuscule basidiomata with yellow to orange-brown colours and minute warts on the pileus, which are only clearly visible with a lens. Microscopically, <em>L. minutoflava</em> is characterized by dextrinoid, ellipsoid to ovoid basidiospores and a pileus covering consisting of chains of spherical cells. Phylogenetically, it is close to <em>L. omninoflava</em> known from China, with a p-distance of 11 % in the ITS sequence. However, <em>L. omninoflava</em> has larger and paler coloured basidiomata, with rather distinctive warts, and a fertile lamella edge (<a href="#R43" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R43">Hou &amp; Ge 2020</a>).</p>
<p>Compared to <em>Lepiota</em> species known from older studies in East and Central Africa and other (sub-)tropical regions, <em>L. minutoflava</em> with its yellow and minute basidiomata resembles only two species, namely <em>L. luteocephalata</em> known from Congo and Uganda (<a href="#R10" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R10">Beeli 1927</a>, <a href="#R76" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R76">Pegler 1977</a>) and <em>L. subflavescens</em> known from Martinique and the USA (<a href="#R70" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R70">Murrill 1914</a>, <a href="#R77" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R77">Pegler 1983</a>). However, <em>L. luteocephalata</em> is distinguished by its sulphur-yellow pileus with brown squamules and white-brownish stipe colouration and <em>L. subflavescens</em> by its brighter lemon-yellow pileus, pale yellow stipe, and the presence of an annulus (<a href="#R10" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R10">Beeli 1927</a>).</p></section><section id="s3_2_2"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">Section <em>Lepiota</em>
</h4>
<p><strong><em>Lepiota woehnertii</em></strong> Sarawi, <strong><em>sp. nov.</em></strong> MB 854016. <a href="#F4" class="usa-link">Fig. 4</a>.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="F4"><h5 class="obj_head">Fig. 4.</h5>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12798316_per-2025-55-9_F4.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/d006/12798316/c0ece2e9341f/per-2025-55-9_F4.jpg" loading="lazy" height="850" width="701" alt="Fig. 4"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/F4/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Basidiomata and microstructures of <em>Lepiota woehnertii</em> (SeSa256, holotype). <strong>A.</strong> Basidiomata. <strong>B.</strong> Basidiospores. <strong>C.</strong> Basidia. <strong>D.</strong> Cheilocystidia. <strong>E.</strong> Pileus covering. Scale bar: A = 1 cm; B = 5 µm; C, D = 10 µm; E = 20 µm.</p></figcaption></figure><p><em>Etymology</em>: Named in honour of Prof. Dr. Jens Wöhnert (Goethe University, Frankfurt am Main), a German biochemist and structural biologist, who kindly supported the present project.</p>
<p><em>Typus</em>: <strong>Benin</strong>, Borgou Department, Forêt de Wari Maro, N9.183, E2.213, alt. 310 m.a.s.l., on sandy soil, in savannah with <em>Isoberlinia doka</em>, 25 Jun. 2022, <em>S. Sarawi, D. Dongnima, F. Hampe, C. Manz &amp; A. Rühl</em>, SeSa256 (<strong>holotype</strong> FR-0175165, <strong>isotype</strong> in UNIPAR). GenBank: ITS = <a href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/nuccore/PP594583" class="usa-link" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">PP594583</a>, LSU = <a href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/nuccore/PP594704" class="usa-link" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">PP594704</a>, <em>RPB2</em> = <a href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/nuccore/PP841212" class="usa-link" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">PP841212</a>.</p>
<p><em>Basidiomata</em> small to medium. <em>Pileus</em> 5–15 mm diam., initially campanulate or hemispherical, then convex and eventually plano-convex, covered by slightly erect, granulate squamules, light orange brown to cream (6B2–3) with a darker brown disc (6D7–8, 6E7, 6F6) in the centre, on a striate, white, background, with white, floccose, velar remnants at outmost margin. <em>Lamellae</em> free, moderately crowded, ventricose, 1–3 mm deep, white edge concolourous; with one lamellula. <em>Stipe</em> 20–25 × 1–2 mm, cylindrical, white to yellowish white (4A2), golden brown (5D4) towards base, smooth, but white woolly floccose below the annular ring zone. <em>Annulus</em> not observed, but annular ring zone present. <em>Odour</em> and <em>taste</em> not recorded. <em>Basidiospores</em> (80/2/2) (10.2–)12.0–13.3–14.6(–17.3) × (2.8–)4.0–4.5–5.0(–6.0) µm, Q = 2.35–3.81, Q<sub>av</sub> = 2.77–3.23, in frontal view fusiform to amygdaliform, in side view with more or less curved abaxial side and adaxial suprahilar depression, hyaline, smooth, dextrinoid, non-metachromatic. <em>Basidia</em> (22–)23–30(–39) × (7–)9–10(–12) µm, clavate, hyaline, with oily content, 4-spored, with up to 5 µm long sterigmata. <em>Lamella edge</em> sterile. <em>Cheilocystidia</em> (22–)25–32(–38) × (6–)8–11(–14) µm, narrowly clavate to clavate or (sub)utriform to fusiform, hyaline. <em>Pleurocystidia</em> absent. <em>Pileus covering</em> a trichoderm consisting of cylindrical, erect cells, (44–)60–107(–155) × (6–)8–12(–15) µm, with rounded apex, with pale brown to yellowish brown parietal pigment, in between with short, narrowly clavate to clavate cells. <em>Clamp connections</em> present in all parts.</p>
<p><em>Ecology, habitat, distribution</em>: Basidiomata solitary or gregarious in small groups, probably saprotrophic, on soil, in gallery forests and savannah. Currently only known from Benin.</p>
<p><em>Toxicity</em>: No amatoxins and phallotoxins were detected.</p>
<p><em>Additional specimen examined</em>: <strong>Benin</strong>, Borgou Department, Tchatchou, Forêt de Tchatchou-Gokana, N9.026, E2.589, alt. 360 m.a.s.l., on sandy soil, in gallery forest with <em>Berlinia grandiflora</em>, 23 Jun. 2022, <em>S. Sarawi, D. Dongnima, F. Hampe, C. Manz &amp; A. Rühl</em>, SeSa242.</p>
<p><em>Notes</em>: <em>Lepiota woehnertii</em>, in section <em>Lepiota</em>, is characterized by basidiomata with light brown to brown squamules on a white background, a striate pileus, and a stipe that is woolly below the ring zone. <em>Lepiota woehnertii</em> is phylogenetically close to the recently described <em>L. pakistanensis</em>, with a p-distance of 4 % in the ITS sequence. However, <em>L. woehnertii</em> differs by its white pileus with a light brown to brown pileus centre and larger basidiospores (12.0–14.6 × 4.0–5.0 µm) in contrast to the ochre and cream colours and distinctly shorter and wider basidiospores (10.4–11.7 × 4.8–5.8 μm) of <em>L. pakistanensis</em> (<a href="#R82" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R82">Rehman <em>et al.</em> 2024</a>). Other morphologically similar species are <em>L. attenuata</em>, known from China and <em>L. metulispora</em>, known from China, India, Sri Lanka, and Tanzania (<a href="#R61" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R61">Liang <em>et al.</em> 2011</a>). <em>Lepiota attenuata</em> differs from <em>L. woehnertii</em> by yellow to yellow brown squamules on the pileus, greyish orange squamules at the base of the stipe, and significantly larger basidiospores (14.5–19.0 × 4.0–5.5 µm) that are distinctively narrower towards apex (<a href="#R13" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R13">Berkeley &amp; Broome 1871</a>, <a href="#R61" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R61">Liang <em>et al.</em> 2011</a>). <em>Lepiota metulispora</em> differs from <em>L. woehnertii</em> by yellowish to yellow-brown colours on the pileus, lemon-yellow to brown colours on the stipe, the presence of an annulus, and slightly smaller basidiospores (13.5–15.5 × 4.5–5.5 µm) (<a href="#R13" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R13">Berkeley &amp; Broome 1871</a>, <a href="#R61" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R61">Liang <em>et al.</em> 2011</a>). However, <a href="#R76" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R76">Pegler (1977)</a> designated this species as <em>L. metulaespora</em>, collected in Tanzania, and provided a description that differed mainly in its larger basidiospores (11–19 × 3.7–5 µm) and its chrome-yellow discolouration when cut. <a href="#R13" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R13">Berkeley and Broome’s (1871)</a> description of <em>L. metulispora</em> included a stuffed stipe, a feature that was contrasted by <a href="#R76" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R76">Pegler (1977)</a> and <a href="#R61" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R61">Liang (2011)</a> who described the stipe as hollow. Therefore, further research is required to verify whether the species collected in Sri Lanka by Berkeley and Broome are distinct species compared to those from Tanzania, China, and India. Morphologically, <em>L. thrombophora</em> from Asia can be confused with <em>L. woehnertii</em>. However, it can be distinguished by the darker, rather dark brown to red-brown colours of its basidiomata (<a href="#R13" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R13">Berkeley &amp; Broome 1871</a>, <a href="#R61" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R61">Liang <em>et al.</em> 2011</a>). Other species within the same clade as <em>L. woehnertii</em> are <em>L. cortinarius</em>, <em>L. magnispora</em>, and <em>L. spheniscispora</em>. These species have larger basidiomata than <em>L. woehnertii</em> and basidiospores strongly differ by their size and shape (<a href="#R69" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R69">Murrill 1912</a>, <a href="#R56" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R56">Lange 1915</a>, <a href="#R103" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R103">Vellinga 2001a</a>, <a href="#R104" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R104">b</a>), and these species occur mainly in temperate regions (viz. Europe or North America).</p></section><section id="s3_2_3"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">Section <em>Lilaceae</em>
</h4>
<p><strong><em>Lepiota beninensis</em></strong> Sarawi &amp; Manz, <strong><em>sp. nov.</em></strong> MB 854017. <a href="#F5" class="usa-link">Fig. 5</a>.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="F5"><h5 class="obj_head">Fig. 5.</h5>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12798316_per-2025-55-9_F5.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/d006/12798316/269599d344af/per-2025-55-9_F5.jpg" loading="lazy" height="853" width="700" alt="Fig. 5"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/F5/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Basidiomata and microstructures of <em>Lepiota beninensis</em> (FC-21-201, holotype). <strong>A.</strong> Basidiomata. <strong>B.</strong> Basidiospores. <strong>C.</strong> Basidia. <strong>D.</strong> Cheilocystidia. <strong>E.</strong> Pileus covering. Scale bar: A = 2 cm; B = 3 µm; C, D = 10 µm; E = 20 µm.</p></figcaption></figure><p><em>Etymology</em>: The epithet <em>beninensis</em> refers to Benin, the country in which this species was collected.</p>
<p><em>Typus</em>: <strong>Benin</strong>, Borgou Department, Béniakou, N9.486, E2.786, alt. 330 m.a.s.l., on loamy rocky soil, in savannah with <em>Isoberlinia tomentosa</em> and <em>Monotes kerstingii</em>, 29 Jun. 2021, <em>C. Manz, F. Hampe &amp; N. S. Yorou</em>, FC-21-201 (<strong>holotype</strong> FR-0175167, <strong>isotype</strong> in UNIPAR). GenBank: ITS = <a href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/nuccore/PP594630" class="usa-link" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">PP594630</a>, LSU = <a href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/nuccore/PP594755" class="usa-link" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">PP594755</a>, <em>RPB2</em> = <a href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/nuccore/PP841161" class="usa-link" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">PP841161</a>.</p>
<p><em>Basidiomata</em> small to rather large and robust. <em>Pileus</em> 15–50 mm diam., convex to plano-convex; centre with a smooth, closed calotte, orange brown to brown or red brown (6C8–3, 6D8–6, 7E8–5, 7F8–5); surface around calotte splitting up into brown to light brown or red-brown (6C6–3, 6D6–4, 7D6–5, 7E6–4) squamules and patches with uplifted tips, on a striate, white to cream (6A1, 6B2) background. <em>Lamellae</em> free, moderately crowded to crowded, ventricose, 2–5 mm deep, white; edge concolourous; lamellulae in up to two tiers. <em>Stipe</em> 10–50 × 2–10 mm, cylindrical, at apex white, light yellow or pale orange (6A2–3, 6B1–4) towards base, with distinct girdles of few squamules at the base that are concolourous with pileus centre. <em>Annulus</em> persistent, white to cream; margin concolourous with pileus centre. <em>Odour</em> and <em>taste</em> not recorded. <em>Basidiospores</em> (80/2/2) (6.4–)7.3–8.0–8.7(–10.3) × (4.3–)4.7–5.1–5.5(–6.0) µm, Q = 1.37–1.81, Q<sub>av</sub> = 1.53–1.60, ellipsoid to ovoid in side view, hyaline, smooth, dextrinoid, non-metachromatic. <em>Basidia</em> (22–)23–29(–36) × (7–)8–10(–11) µm, narrowly clavate to clavate, hyaline, with oily content, 4-spored, with up to 5 µm long sterigmata. <em>Lamella edge</em> sterile. <em>Cheilocystidia</em> (15–)20–33(–42) × (6–)7–10(–12) µm, clavate, subcapitate to utriform, hyaline. <em>Pleurocystidia</em> absent. <em>Pileus covering</em> a hymeniderm consisting of clavate to narrowly clavate, sometimes irregularly shaped cells, (21–)30–51(–67) × (7–)9–14(–21) µm, with brown parietal pigment, some incrusting pigment present in the subpellis. <em>Clamp connections</em> present in all parts.</p>
<p><em>Ecology, habitat, and distribution</em>: Basidiomata solitary, probably saprotrophic, on soil, in savannah. Currently only known from Benin.</p>
<p><em>Toxicity</em>: No amatoxins and phallotoxins were detected.</p>
<p><em>Additional specimen examined</em>: <strong>Benin</strong>, Borgou Department, near Kpessou, Forêt de l’Ouémé Supérieur, N 9.261 E 2.184, alt. 340 m.a.s.l., on sandy soil, in savannah with <em>Isoberlinia doka</em> and <em>I. tomentosa</em>, 28 Jun. 2022, <em>S. Sarawi, D. Dongnima, F. Hampe, C. Manz &amp; A. Rühl</em>, SeSa296.</p>
<p><em>Notes</em>: <em>Lepiota beninensis</em>, section <em>Lilacea</em>, is characterized by robust basidiomata with an orange-brown calotte at the pileus centre, which splits into brown to light-brown squamules and patches on a white background, along with an annulus with brown margin, dextrinoid, ellipsoid to ovoid basidiospores, and a hymeniderm as pileus covering. Phylogenetically, <em>L. beninensis</em> forms a distinct clade together with <em>L. chiangraiensis</em>, <em>L. bengalensis</em>, and <em>L. ochraceofulva</em>. Macroscopically, <em>L. chiangraiensis</em> known from Thailand and <em>L. bengalensis</em> known from Bangladesh, can be easily distinguished from <em>L. beninensis</em> by the less intense orange colours on pileus and stipe, as well as yellowish to yellowish white lamellae (<a href="#R42" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R42">Hosen <em>et al.</em> 2016</a>, <a href="#R46" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R46">Hyde <em>et al.</em> 2020</a>). Microscopically, the basidiospores of <em>L. beninensis</em> (7.3–8.7 × 4.7–5.5 µm) are slightly smaller than the non-dextrinoid basidiospores of <em>L. chiangraiensis</em> (8.5–10.2 × 5–6.2 μm), and larger than those of <em>L. bengalensis</em> (6.0–6.6 × 3.0–3.6 μm) (<a href="#R42" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R42">Hosen <em>et al.</em> 2016</a>, <a href="#R46" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R46">Hyde <em>et al.</em> 2020</a>). <em>Lepiota beninensis</em> can be differentiated from the European species <em>L. ochraceofulva</em> which has less yellowish to ochraceous colours on the pileus and stipe, and it lacks a persistent annulus (<a href="#R74" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R74">Orton 1960</a>, <a href="#R103" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R103">Vellinga 2001a</a>).</p>
<p><strong><em>Lepiota lilaceostriata</em></strong> Sarawi, <strong><em>sp. nov.</em></strong> MB 854018. <a href="#F6" class="usa-link">Fig. 6</a>.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="F6"><h5 class="obj_head">Fig. 6.</h5>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12798316_per-2025-55-9_F6.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/d006/12798316/768952a6d29d/per-2025-55-9_F6.jpg" loading="lazy" height="831" width="701" alt="Fig. 6"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/F6/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Basidiomata and microstructures of <em>Lepiota lilaceostriata</em>. <strong>A.</strong> Basidioma (SeSa280, holotype), seen from above (left) and from below the pileus (right). <strong>B.</strong> Basidioma (SeSa241). <strong>C.</strong> Basidiospores (SeSa280). <strong>D.</strong> Basidia (SeSa280). <strong>E.</strong> Cheilocystidia (SeSa280). <strong>F.</strong> Pileus covering (SeSa280). Scale bar: A, B = 7 mm; C = 2 µm; D, E = 10 µm; F = 20 µm.</p></figcaption></figure><p><em>Etymology</em>: <em>lilaceus</em> (Latin) = lilac-coloured, <em>striatus</em> (Latin) = striate; refers to the purplish colours, which are similar to those of <em>Lepiota lilacea</em>, and the striate pileus.</p>
<p><em>Typus</em>: <strong>Benin</strong>, Atacora Department, Kota waterfalls, N10.212, E1.444, alt. 500 m.a.s.l., on sandy soil, in savannah with <em>Berlinia grandiflora</em>, <em>Isoberlinia tomentosa</em>, and <em>Uapaca guinensis</em>, 26 Jun. 2022, <em>S. Sarawi, D. Dongnima, F. Hampe, C. Manz &amp; A. Rühl</em>, SeSa280 (<strong>holotype</strong> FR-0175191, <strong>isotype</strong> in UNIPAR). GenBank: ITS = <a href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/nuccore/PP594588" class="usa-link" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">PP594588</a>, LSU = <a href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/nuccore/PP594709" class="usa-link" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">PP594709</a>, <em>RPB2</em> = <a href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/nuccore/PP841217" class="usa-link" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">PP841217</a>.</p>
<p><em>Basidiomata</em> small. <em>Pileus</em> 10–15 mm diam., umbonate or convex to plano-convex; centre with a smooth, closed calotte, dark purple, almost black to purplish grey (14F2–3) or brownish grey to grey (8E1–2); surface around calotte splitting up into purplish squamules with uplifted tips, on a striate, white to cream (8A1, 8B1) background. <em>Lamellae</em> free, moderately crowded to crowded, ventricose, 1–2 mm deep, white edge concolourous; with one lamellula. <em>Stipe</em> 20 × 1–2 mm, cylindrical, annulate, greyish yellow (4C3) above the ring, olive-brown towards base (4D3). <em>Annulus</em> persistent; underside concolorous with pileus centre; upper side whitish to cream. <em>Odour</em> and <em>taste</em> not recorded. <em>Basidiospores</em> (80/2/2) (3.6–)4.0–4.4–4.8(–5.4) × (2.1–)2.6–2.9–3.2(–3.7) µm, Q = 1.26–1.73, Q<sub>av</sub> = 1.44–1.57, ellipsoid to ovoid in side view, hyaline, smooth, non-dextrinoid, non-metachromatic. <em>Basidia</em> (13–)16–19(–21) × (5.0–)5.3–6.3(–7.3) µm, narrowly clavate to clavate, hyaline, with oily content, 4-spored, with up to 3 µm long sterigmata. <em>Lamella edge</em> sterile. <em>Cheilocystidia</em> (15–)17–23(–27) × (7–)8–11(–14) µm, clavate to broadly clavate, hyaline. <em>Pleurocystidia</em> absent. <em>Pileus covering</em> a hymeniderm consisting of cylindrical to narrowly clavate cells, (11–)17–31(–45) × (4–)6–11(–13) µm, hyaline, lacking any pigment; young cells with light brown, and hyphae of the underlying layer with brown parietal pigment. <em>Clamp connections</em> present in all parts.</p>
<p><em>Ecology, habitat, and distribution</em>: Basidiomata solitary, probably saprotrophic, on soil and decayed wood, in rather moist tree savannah and gallery forests. Currently only known from Benin.</p>
<p><em>Toxicity</em>: No amatoxins and phallotoxins were detected.</p>
<p><em>Additional specimen examined</em>: <strong>Benin</strong>, Borgou Department, Tchatchou, Forêt de Tchatchou-Gokana, N9.026, E2.589, alt. 360 m.a.s.l., on decayed wood, in gallery forest with <em>Berlinia grandiflora</em>, 23 Jun. 2022, <em>S. Sarawi, D. Dongnima, F. Hampe, C. Manz &amp; A. Rühl</em>, SeSa241.</p>
<p><em>Notes</em>: <em>Lepiota lilaceostriata</em>, section <em>Lilaceae</em>, is characterized by a striate cap with a dark purple to black, sometimes rather brownish grey calotte, which breaks up into radial patches towards the edge, an annulus which is coloured on the underside, and non-dextrinoid basidiospores. This species resembles <em>L. lilacea</em>, known from America and Europe, due to the pileus colour and the coloured annulus, as well as non-dextrinoid ellipsoid to ovoid basidiospores and a hymeniderm as pileus covering (<a href="#R17" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R17">Bresadola 1892</a>, <a href="#R103" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R103">Vellinga 2001a</a>). Distinguishing these two species morphologically can be challenging; however, <em>L. lilacea</em> typically displays a less striate cap and fleshier basidiomata compared to <em>L. lilaceostriata</em> (<a href="#R17" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R17">Bresadola 1892</a>, <a href="#R103" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R103">Vellinga 2001a</a>). Microscopically, there are no evident differences. Phylogenetic studies suggest that the species most closely related to <em>L. lilaceostriata</em> is <em>L. lilacea</em>, with a p-distance of 8 % in the ITS sequence. These two species form a cluster, distinct from all other representatives of the section <em>Lilaceae</em>. To differentiate <em>L. lilaceostriata</em> from <em>L. lilacea</em> conclusively, consulting molecular sequence data is recommended. Other species resembling <em>L. lilaceostriata</em> include <em>L. felina</em> and <em>L. pseudolilacea</em>, which also have an annulus that is coloured on the underside (<a href="#R49" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R49">Karsten 1879</a>, <a href="#R45" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R45">Huijsman 1947</a>). However, they differ in the trichodermal pileus covering and basidiospore size, and are phylogenetically distant, belonging to section <em>Lepiota</em> (<a href="#R103" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R103">Vellinga 2001a</a>, <a href="#R106" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R106">2003</a>, <a href="#R90" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R90">Sarawi <em>et al.</em> 2025</a>).</p></section><section id="s3_2_4"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">Section <em>Helveolae</em>
</h4>
<p><strong><em>Lepiota minutisterigmata</em></strong> Sarawi, <strong><em>sp. nov.</em></strong> MB 854019. <a href="#F7" class="usa-link">Fig. 7</a>.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="F7"><h5 class="obj_head">Fig. 7.</h5>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12798316_per-2025-55-9_F7.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/d006/12798316/edd831b19c4f/per-2025-55-9_F7.jpg" loading="lazy" height="750" width="701" alt="Fig. 7"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/F7/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Basidiomata and microstructures of <em>Lepiota minutisterigmata</em> (SeSa343, holotype). <strong>A.</strong> Basidiomata. <strong>B.</strong> Basidiospores. <strong>C.</strong> Basidia. <strong>D.</strong> Cheilocystidia. <strong>E.</strong> Pileus covering. Scale bar: A = 1 cm; B = 3 µm; C, D = 10 µm; E = 20 µm.</p></figcaption></figure><p><em>Etymology</em>: <em>minutus</em> (Latin) = very small, <em>sterigma</em> (Greek) = small, stalk-like structure; refers to the small sterigmata of this species.</p>
<p><em>Typus</em>: <strong>Benin</strong>, Collines Department, between Djalloukou and Konkon, N7.730, E1.839, alt. 190 m.a.s.l., on sandy soil, in woodland with <em>Isoberlinia doka</em>, 3 Jul. 2022, <em>S. Sarawi, D. Dongnima, F. Hampe, C. Manz &amp; A. Rühl</em>, SeSa343 (<strong>holotype</strong> FR-0175197, <strong>isotype</strong> in UNIPAR) GenBank: ITS = <a href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/nuccore/PP594606" class="usa-link" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">PP594606</a>, LSU = <a href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/nuccore/PP594729" class="usa-link" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">PP594729</a>, <em>RPB2</em> = <a href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/nuccore/PP841238" class="usa-link" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">PP841238</a>.</p>
<p><em>Basidiomata</em> small to medium. <em>Pileus</em> 5–15 mm diam., convex to plano-convex, with umbo, covered by concentrically arranged, pyramidal, brown (6F8–3, 6E8–5) squamules on a light brown to white (6A1–2, 6C2–4, 6D3–5) background. <em>Lamellae</em> free, moderately crowded to crowded, ventricose, 1–3 mm deep, white; edge concolourous; with one lamellula. <em>Stipe</em> 15–30 × 1–2 mm, cylindrical, at apex white floccose, red-golden to light brown (6C4–2, 6D4–2, 7D5–3) towards base. <em>Annulus</em> not observed. <em>Odour</em> and <em>taste</em> not recorded. <em>Basidiospores</em> (80/2/2) (5.1–)6.4–7.0–7.6(–8.2) × (3.5–)4.8–5.3–5.9(–6.4) µm, Q = 1.12–1.49, Q<sub>av</sub> = 1.24–1.39, ellipsoid to ovoid, hyaline, smooth, dextrinoid, non-metachromatic. <em>Basidia</em> (15–)18–24(–29) × (8–)9–11(–12) µm, narrowly clavate to clavate, hyaline, with oily content, 4-spored, rarely 2-spored, mostly with short, up to 0.5(–1) µm long sterigmata, occasionally up to 4 µm long. <em>Lamella edge</em> sterile. <em>Cheilocystidia</em> (12–)16–27(–43) × (6–)7–12(–21) µm, narrowly clavate or clavate to broadly clavate or slightly inflated, hyaline. <em>Pleurocystidia</em> absent. <em>Pileus covering</em> a trichoderm consisting of inflated clavate cells, (18–)28–46(–57) × (9–)14–21(–26) µm, with pale brown parietal pigment. <em>Clamp connections</em> present in all parts.</p>
<p><em>Ecology, habitat, and distribution</em>: Basidiomata solitary or gregarious in small groups, probably saprotrophic, on soil, in woodland and savannah. Currently only known from Benin.</p>
<p><em>Toxicity</em>: No amatoxins and phallotoxins were detected.</p>
<p><em>Additional specimen examined</em>: <strong>Benin</strong>, Atacora Department, Kota waterfalls, N10.212, E1.444, alt. 500 m.a.s.l., on sandy soil, in savannah with <em>Berlinia grandiflora</em>, <em>Isoberlinia tomentosa</em>, and <em>Uapaca guinensis</em>, 26 Jun. 2022, <em>S. Sarawi, D. Dongnima, F. Hampe, C. Manz &amp; A. Rühl</em>, SeSa281.</p>
<p><em>Notes</em>: This species is characterized by light brown to brown colours and pyramidal squamules on a white background, ellipsoid to ovoid basidiospores, basidia with predominantly minute sterigmata and a trichoderm formed by inflated cells. <em>Lepiota minutisterigmata</em> forms a clade together with the tropical species <em>L. lahorensis</em>, <em>L. sosuensis</em>, <em>L. vellingana</em>, and the European species <em>L. farinolens</em>. The relationships within this clade are not resolved. <em>Lepiota minutisterigmata</em> differs from <em>L. lahorensis</em> by its pyramidal squamules and basidiospores that are smaller (6.4–7.6 × 4.8–5.9 µm) than those of <em>L. lahorensis</em> (8.3–11.6 × 6.1–8.3 μm) (<a href="#R80" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R80">Qasim <em>et al.</em> 2016</a>). Macroscopically, <em>L. sosuensis</em> can be easily distinguished from <em>L. minutisterigmata</em> by pure white basidiomata and the lack of pyramidal squamules (<a href="#R47" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R47">Justo <em>et al.</em> 2015</a>). <em>Lepiota vellingana</em> differs from <em>L. minutisterigmata</em> by lacking pyramidal squamules and the absence of an annulus (<a href="#R71" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R71">Nawaz <em>et al.</em> 2013</a>). Additionally, <em>L. minutisterigmata</em> differs by short sterigmata and a pileus covering consisting of short, inflated cells (<a href="#R71" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R71">Nawaz <em>et al.</em> 2013</a>, <a href="#R47" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R47">Justo <em>et al.</em> 2015</a>, <a href="#R80" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R80">Qasim <em>et al.</em> 2016</a>). The European species <em>L. farinolens</em> can be distinguished macroscopically from <em>L. minutisterigmata</em> by basidiomata with ochre to pink to pink-brown colours, plane squamules, and an annulus. Microscopically it differs by slightly larger basidiospores (8.0–9.0 × 4.5–5.0 µm), significantly larger cheilocystidia (40–50 × 8–12 µm), and much longer and narrower cells of the pileus covering (100–160 × 10–15) (<a href="#R15" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R15">Bon &amp; Riousset 1992</a>).</p>
<p><strong><em>Lepiota pseudovenenosa</em></strong> Sarawi &amp; Reschke, <strong><em>sp. nov.</em></strong> MB 854020. <a href="#F8" class="usa-link">Fig. 8</a>.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="F8"><h5 class="obj_head">Fig. 8.</h5>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12798316_per-2025-55-9_F8.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/d006/12798316/0bbd3a7ca738/per-2025-55-9_F8.jpg" loading="lazy" height="824" width="699" alt="Fig. 8"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/F8/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Basidiomata and microstructures of <em>Lepiota pseudovenenosa</em>. <strong>A.</strong> Basidiomata (SeSa302, holotype). <strong>B.</strong> Basidiomata (SeSa339). <strong>C.</strong> Basidiospores (SeSa302). <strong>D.</strong> Basidia (SeSa302). <strong>E.</strong> Cheilocystidia (SeSa302). <strong>F.</strong> Pileus covering (SeSa302). Scale bar: A = 1 cm; B = 2 cm; C = 2 µm; D, E = 10 µm; F = 20 µm.</p></figcaption></figure><p><em>Etymology</em>: <em>pseudes</em> (Greek) = deceptive, <em>venenosa</em> (Latin) = poisonous; refers to the species’ appearance, which is similar to poisonous <em>Lepiota</em> species, such as <em>L. subincarnata</em> and <em>L. brunneoincarnata</em>, yet this species does not contain amatoxins or phallotoxins.</p>
<p><em>Typus</em>: <strong>Benin</strong>, Donga Department, Bassila, N9.001, E1.649, alt. 360 m.a.s.l., on sandy soil, in dense gallery forest with <em>Berlinia grandiflora</em>, 30 Jun. 2022, <em>S. Sarawi, R. Dramani, F. Hampe, C. Manz &amp; A. Rühl</em>, SeSa302 (<strong>holotype</strong> FR-0175172, <strong>isotype</strong> in UNIPAR). GenBank: ITS = <a href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/nuccore/PP594593" class="usa-link" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">PP594593</a>, LSU = <a href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/nuccore/PP594714" class="usa-link" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">PP594714</a>, <em>RPB2</em> = <a href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/nuccore/PP841223" class="usa-link" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">PP841223</a>.</p>
<p><em>Basidiomata</em> small to medium. <em>Pileus</em> 10–25 mm diam., first campanulate to hemispherical or conical, becoming convex to plano-convex or plano-concave, with umbo; at centre dark brown to nearly black (7F7–8, 8F7–8) to sometimes vinaceous pink, dark pink or red-brown (9F7–8, 10F7–8), paler towards margin (7D5–8, 7E7–8, 9D5–7, 9E6–8, 10D5–7), covered by concentrically arranged, pyramidal or tomentose squamules that are concolourous with pileus centre, on a pale brown to vinaceous pink or white background. <em>Lamellae</em> free, moderately crowded to crowded, ventricose, 1–2 mm deep, white; edge concolourous; lamellulae in up to two tiers. <em>Stipe</em> 10–25 × 2–3 mm, cylindrical, annulate, whitish to cream above the annulus, gradually darker, from pale brown or pale vinaceous pink to reddish brown, dark brown or vinaceous pink to dark pink (7D4–8, 7E7–8, 7F6–8, 9D4–7, 9E6–8, 9F7–8, 10D5–7, 10F7–8) towards base, smooth. <em>Annnulus</em> non-persistent; margin concolourous with pileus centre; upper side whitish to cream. <em>Odour</em> and <em>taste</em> not recorded. <em>Basidiospores</em> (200/5/5) (3.0–)4.0–4.4–4.7(–5.4) × (2.0–)2.4–2.7–3.1(–3.8) µm, Q = 1.32–1.95, Q<sub>av</sub> = 1.53–1.71, ellipsoid to ovoid, hyaline, smooth, non-dextrinoid or at most weakly dextrinoid, non-metachromatic. <em>Basidia</em> (14–)16–19(–23) × (4.0–)5.0–6.5(–8.0) µm, narrowly clavate to clavate, hyaline, with oily content, 4-spored, occasionally 2-spored, with up to 2–3 µm long sterigmata. <em>Lamella edge</em> sterile. <em>Cheilocystidia</em> (12–)17–25(–36) × (3.0–)4.5–6.3(–7.9) µm, cylindrical or narrowly clavate to clavate, hyaline. <em>Pleurocystidia</em> absent. <em>Pileus covering</em> a trichoderm consisting of cylindrical, erect cells, (12–)42–93(–153) × (3.6–)5.5–8.7(–12.1) µm, with rounded apex, with pale brown to brown parietal pigment. <em>Clamp connections</em> present in all parts.</p>
<p><em>Ecology, habitat, and distribution</em>: Basidiomata solitary or gregarious in small groups of three to six basidiomata, probably saprotrophic, on soil and dead wood, in gallery forests. Currently known from Benin, as well as the Dominican Republic and Panama according to GenBank sequences.</p>
<p><em>Toxicity</em>: No amatoxins and phallotoxins were detected.</p>
<p><em>Additional specimens examined</em>: <strong>Benin</strong>, Donga Department, Bassila, N9.001, E1.649, alt. 360 m.a.s.l., on sandy soil, in dense gallery forest with <em>Berlinia grandiflora</em>, 30 Jun. 2022, <em>S. Sarawi, R. Dramani, F. Hampe, C. Manz &amp; A. Rühl</em>, SeSa303; ibid., on sandy soil, in dense gallery forest with <em>Berlinia grandiflora</em>, 30 Jun. 2022, <em>S. Sarawi, R. Dramani, F. Hampe, C. Manz &amp; A. Rühl</em>, SeSa304; ibid., on sandy soil, in dense gallery forest with <em>Berlinia grandiflora</em>, 30 Jun. 2022, <em>S. Sarawi, R. Dramani, F. Hampe, C. Manz, A. Rühl</em>, SeSa309; ibid., on dead wood, in dense gallery forest with <em>Berlinia grandiflora</em>, 2 Jul. 2022, <em>S. Sarawi, R. Dramani, F. Hampe, C. Manz &amp; A. Rühl</em>, SeSa339.</p>
<p><em>Notes</em>: <em>Lepiota pseudovenenosa</em> in section <em>Helveolae</em>, is characterized by light brown, brown to pink- or red-brown colours, pyramidal squamules in the pileus centre, which flatten towards margin, an annulus with coloured margin, non-dextrinoid, ellipsoid to ovoid basidiospores, narrow cheilocystidia and a trichoderm as pileus covering.
