The closest related species is <em>L. brunneodisca</em>, with a p-distance of 13 % in the ITS sequence. <em>Lepiota brunneodisca</em>, known from India, differs from <em>L. pseudovenenosa</em> by light brown to orange brown or yellowish white colours, appressed squamules, and an annulus without coloured margin (<a href="#R84" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R84">Roy <em>et al.</em> 2023</a>). The basidiospores of <em>L. pseudovenenosa</em> (4.0–4.7 × 2.4–3.1 µm) are smaller than those of <em>L. brunneodisca</em> (6.2–7.7 × 3.8–4.5 µm) and the cheilocystidia of <em>L. pseudovenenosa</em> are narrower (<a href="#R84" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R84">Roy <em>et al.</em> 2023</a>). The non-dextrinoid basidiospores of <em>L. pseudovenenosa</em> are a distinctive characteristic to differentiate it from other representatives of section <em>Helveolae</em>, as no species with non-dextrinoid basidiospores were known within this subsection up to now. However, the dextrinoidity of <em>L. brunneodisca</em> has not been examined, thus it cannot be used here as a characteristic to distinguish the species (<a href="#R84" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R84">Roy <em>et al.</em> 2023</a>).</p>
<p>Morphologically, two further species, <em>L. erinana</em>, known from Trinidad and <em>L. flagellata</em>, known from Kenya, Sri Lanka, and Uganda, resemble <em>L. pseudovenenosa</em>. However, <em>L. erinana</em> differs from <em>L. pseudovenosa</em> by the absence of an annulus and a scaly stipe (<a href="#R26" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R26">Dennis 1970</a>) and <em>L. flagellata</em> differs by the absence of an annulus, dextrinoid basidiospores, and inflated cheilocystidia (<a href="#R76" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R76">Pegler 1977</a>).</p></section><section id="s3_2_5"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">Section <em>Stenosporae</em>
</h4>
<p><strong><em>Lepiota aurantiicolor</em></strong> Sarawi, <strong><em>sp. nov.</em></strong> MB 854021. <a href="#F9" class="usa-link">Fig. 9</a>.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="F9"><h5 class="obj_head">Fig. 9.</h5>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12798316_per-2025-55-9_F9.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/d006/12798316/b6bab59343ce/per-2025-55-9_F9.jpg" loading="lazy" height="584" width="701" alt="Fig. 9"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/F9/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Microstructures of <em>Lepiota aurantiicolor</em> (SeSa176, holotype). <strong>A.</strong> Basidiospores. <strong>B.</strong> Basidia. <strong>C.</strong> Cheilocystidia. <strong>D.</strong> Pileus covering. Scale bar: A = 3 µm; B, C = 10 µm; D = 20 µm.</p></figcaption></figure><p><em>Etymology</em>: <em>aurantius</em> (Latin) = golden, <em>color</em> (Latin) = colour; refers to the colours of the pileus.</p>
<p><em>Typus</em>: <strong>Benin</strong>, Collines Department, near Kilibo, N8.616, E2.665, alt. 320 m.a.s.l., on sandy soil, in woodland dominated by <em>Isoberlinia doka</em> and <em>Uapaca togoensis</em>, 21 Jun. 2022, <em>S. Sarawi, D. Dongnima, F. Hampe, C. Manz &amp; A. Rühl</em>, SeSa176 (<strong>holotype</strong> FR-0175175, <strong>isotype</strong> in UNIPAR). GenBank: ITS = <a href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/nuccore/PP594563" class="usa-link" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">PP594563</a>, LSU = <a href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/nuccore/PP594681" class="usa-link" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">PP594681</a>, <em>RPB2</em> = <a href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/nuccore/PP841188" class="usa-link" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">PP841188</a>.</p>
<p><em>Basidiomata</em> small to medium. <em>Pileus</em> 5–20 mm diam., first hemispherical to conical and then becoming convex to plano-convex or plano-concave, covered by slightly erect, tomentose squamules, at centre orange brown or reddish brown to brown (7C7–8, 7D7–8, 7E7–8, 8D7–8, 8E7–8), light orange, light orange red or light brown (7A5–7, 7B5–7, 7C5–7, 7D4–7) towards margin, on a white background, with velar remnants at outmost margin. <em>Lamellae</em> free, moderately crowded to crowded, ventricose, 1–4 mm deep, white to cream edge concolourous; lamellulae in up to two tiers. <em>Stipe</em> 10–35 × 1–3 mm, cylindrical, at apex white to cream, gradually more intensely coloured, pale yellow, pale orange, orange-brown or reddish brown (5A2–4, 5B3–5, 6A3–5, 6B4–6, 7B3–5, 7C5–7, 7D4–8) towards base, in lower half with distinct girdles of few squamules that are concolourous with pileus centre. <em>Annulus</em> not observed. <em>Odour</em> and <em>taste</em> not recorded. <em>Basidiospores</em> (200/5/5) (4.9–)6.0–6.7–7.4(–8.4) × (2.4–)3.1–3.5–3.9(–4.6) µm, Q = 1.58–2.26, Q<sub>av</sub> = 1.78–2.03, ellipsoid in front view and subtriangular in side view, with an indistinct spur, hyaline, smooth, dextrinoid, non-metachromatic. <em>Basidia</em> (15–)17–20(–23) × (6.1–)7.0– 8.1(–8.6) µm, narrowly clavate to clavate, hyaline, with oily content, 4-spored, frequently 2- or 1-spored, with up to 3–6 µm long, straight, non-bulbous sterigmata, when 2- or 1-spored with up to 10 µm long, irregularly shaped, bulbous sterigmata. <em>Lamella edge</em> sterile. <em>Cheilocystidia</em> (16–)19–26(–31) × (5–)6–7(–8) µm, narrowly clavate to clavate or cylindrical, occasionally subutriform to narrowly utriform, hyaline. <em>Pleurocystidia</em> absent. <em>Pileus covering</em> a trichoderm made up of (37–)46–81(–118) × (8–)10–14(–19) µm, clavate or cylindrical, erect cells, with rounded apex, with pale brown to brown parietal pigment. <em>Clamp connections</em> present in all parts.</p>
<p><em>Ecology, habitat, and distribution</em>: Basidiomata solitary or gregarious, probably saprotrophic, on soil, in woodland. Currently only known from one locality in Benin.</p>
<p><em>Toxicity</em>: No amatoxins and phallotoxins were detected.</p>
<p><em>Additional specimens examined</em>: <strong>Benin</strong>, Collines Department, near Kilibo, N8.616, E2.665, alt. 320 m.a.s.l., on sandy soil, in woodland dominated by <em>Isoberlinia doka</em> and <em>Uapaca togoensis</em>, 21 Jun. 2022, <em>S. Sarawi, D. Dongnima, F. Hampe, C. Manz &amp; A. Rühl</em>, SeSa177; ibid., in woodland dominated by <em>Isoberlinia doka</em> and <em>Uapaca togoensis</em>, 21 Jun. 2022, <em>S. Sarawi, D. Dongnima, F. Hampe, C. Manz &amp; A. Rühl</em>, SeSa181; ibid., on sandy soil, in woodland dominated by <em>Isoberlinia doka</em> and <em>Uapaca togoensis</em>, 21 Jun. 2022, <em>S. Sarawi, D. Dongnima, F. Hampe, C. Manz &amp; A. Rühl</em>, SeSa184; ibid., on sandy soil, in woodland dominated by <em>Isoberlinia doka</em> and <em>Uapaca togoensis</em>, 22 Jun. 2022, <em>S. Sarawi, D. Dongnima, F. Hampe, C. Manz &amp; A. Rühl</em>, SeSa216.</p>
<p><em>Notes</em>: <em>Lepiota aurantiicolor</em>, section <em>Stenosporae</em>, is characterized by orange brown basidiomata, indistinctively spurred basidiospores, mostly 4-spored basidia, occasionally 2-spored with longer sterigmata, and a trichodermal pileus covering. <em>Lepiota aurantiicolor</em> has strong macroscopic similarity to <em>L. longisterigmata</em> and <em>L. flavostipitata</em> and often co-occurs in the same habitat. Accurate identification mainly relies on microscopic characteristics of the sterigmata and molecular sequence data. <em>Lepiota longisterigmata</em> can be easily distinguished from <em>L. aurantiicolor</em> by its long sterigmata. <em>Lepiota flavostipitata</em> differs from <em>L. aurantiicolor</em> by deeper, darker orange-brown colours and a yellowish stipe apex. Further related species are <em>L. alopochroa</em> and <em>L. brunneopileata</em>. <em>Lepiota alopochroa</em>, known from New Zealand and Thailand, can be differentiated from <em>L. aurantiicolor</em> by serrulate, yellow to orange-white lamellae and larger basidiospores (7.5–9.3 × 3.8–4.0 µm) compared to <em>L. aurantiicolor</em> with basidiospores measuring (6.0–7.4 × 3.1–3.9 µm) (<a href="#R99" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R99">Sysouphanthong <em>et al.</em> 2011</a>). Furthermore, the lighter coloration of <em>L. aurantiicolor</em> and the non-metachromatic basidiospores differentiate it from <em>L. brunneopileata</em>, known from Pakistan (<a href="#R82" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R82">Rehman <em>et al.</em> 2024</a>). Given the complexity of this species complex, molecular sequence data are recommended for accurate identification.</p>
<p>Further, <em>L. aurantiicolor</em> morphologically resembles <em>L. pseudoignicolor</em>, known from Guadeloupe and Venezuela, by the colours of the basidiomata and the basidiospore shape and size, but lacks molecular sequence data. However, <em>L. pseudoignicolor</em> differs by yellow lamellae and larger basidiospores (9–11.5 × 3–4 µm) (<a href="#R26" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R26">Dennis 1970</a>, <a href="#R77" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R77">Pegler 1983</a>).</p>
<p><strong><em>Lepiota brunneoolivacea</em></strong> Sarawi, <strong><em>sp. nov.</em></strong> MB 854022. <a href="#F10" class="usa-link">Fig. 10</a>.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="F10"><h5 class="obj_head">Fig. 10.</h5>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12798316_per-2025-55-9_F10.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/d006/12798316/6f4290414572/per-2025-55-9_F10.jpg" loading="lazy" height="849" width="699" alt="Fig. 10"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/F10/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Basidiomata and microstructures of <em>Lepiota brunneoolivacea</em> (SeSa214, holotype). <strong>A.</strong> Basidioma, seen from above the pileus. <strong>B.</strong> Basidioma, seen from below the pileus. <strong>C.</strong> Basidiospores. <strong>D.</strong> Basidia. <strong>E.</strong> Cheilocystidia. <strong>F.</strong> Pileus covering. Scale bar: A, B = 8 mm; C = 3 µm; D, E = 10 µm; F = 20 µm.</p></figcaption></figure><p><em>Etymology</em>: <em>brunneus</em> (Latin) = brown, <em>olivaceus</em> (Latin) = olive-coloured; refers to the brown to olive brown basidiomata.</p>
<p><em>Typus</em>: <strong>Benin</strong>, Collines Department, near Kilibo, N8.616, E2.665, alt. 320 m.a.s.l., on sandy soil, in woodland dominated by <em>Isoberlinia doka</em> and <em>Uapaca togoensis</em>, 22 Jun. 2022, <em>S. Sarawi, D. Dongnima, F. Hampe, C. Manz &amp; A. Rühl</em>, SeSa214 (<strong>holotype</strong> FR-0175163, <strong>isotype</strong> in UNIPAR). GenBank: ITS = <a href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/nuccore/PP594574" class="usa-link" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">PP594574</a>, LSU = <a href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/nuccore/PP594693" class="usa-link" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">PP594693</a>, <em>RPB2 =</em> <a href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/nuccore/PP841201" class="usa-link" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">PP841201</a>.</p>
<p><em>Basidiomata</em> small to medium. <em>Pileus</em> 5–15 mm diam., convex to plano-convex, with or without umbo, at centre with a dark yellow brown (5F8–4) calotte; surface around calotte splitting up into yellow brown to light brown or olive brown (5D8–4, 5E8–4) patches, towards the margin sometimes with a slight red tinge, on a light yellow brown (5D4) background. <em>Lamellae</em> free, moderately crowded to crowded, ventricose, 2–4 mm deep, white to cream, discolouring brownish red with age; edge concolourous; lamellulae in up to two tiers. <em>Stipe</em> 15–35 × 2–3 mm, cylindrical, at apex yellow brown (5E8–4, 5D8–4), more brown to reddish brown (6F8–4, 7F8–4, 8F8–6) towards base. <em>Annulus</em> not observed. <em>Odour</em> and <em>taste</em> not recorded. <em>Basidiospores</em> (80/2/2) (5.4–)6.0–6.6–7.1(–7.8) × (2.5–)2.9–3.2–3.5(–4.2) µm, Q = 1.69–2.48, Q<sub>av</sub> = 2.05–2.08, amygdaliform in front view to triangular in side view, with an indistinct spur, hyaline, smooth, dextrinoid, non-metachromatic. <em>Basidia</em> (15–)17–20(–24) × (5.6–)6.3–7.5(–8.5) µm, narrowly clavate to clavate, hyaline, with oily content, 4-spored, occasionally 2-spored, with up to 4 µm long sterigmata. <em>Lamella edge</em> sterile. <em>Cheilocystidia</em> (13–)19–25(–29) × (5–)6–9(–11) µm, narrowly clavate to clavate or narrowly fusiform, occasionally subutriform to narrowly utriform, rarely septate, hyaline. <em>Pleurocystidia</em> absent. <em>Pileus covering</em> a trichoderm made up of (46–)63–108(–131) × (8–)10–15(–18) µm, clavate or cylindrical, erect cells, with rounded apex, with pale brown to brown parietal pigment. <em>Clamp connections</em> present in all parts.</p>
<p><em>Ecology, habitat, and distribution</em>: Basidiomata solitary, probably saprotrophic on, soil, in woodland and savannah. Currently only known from Benin.</p>
<p><em>Toxicity</em>: No amatoxins and phallotoxins were detected.</p>
<p><em>Additional specimen examined</em>: <strong>Benin</strong>, Atacora Department, Kota waterfalls, N10.212, E1.444, alt. 500 m.a.s.l., on sandy soil, in savannah with <em>Berlinia grandiflora</em>, <em>Isoberlinia tomentosa</em>, and <em>Uapaca guinensis</em>, 17 Jun. 2022, <em>S. Sarawi, D. Dongnima, F. Hampe, C. Manz &amp; A. Rühl</em>, SeSa137.</p>
<p><em>Notes</em>: <em>Lepiota brunneoolivacea</em> in section <em>Stenosporae</em>, is characterized by yellow-brown, light brown to olive-brown basidiomata, with a stipe that is reddish brown towards the base, dextrinoid, indistinctively spurred basidiospores, and a trichodermal pileus covering. Phylogenetically, <em>L. brunneoolivacea</em> clusters together with further species which form basidiomata with greenish colours, but the support of this clade is not significant and further studies are needed to analyse the phylogeny of those species. Species closely related to <em>L. brunneoolivacea</em> include <em>L. grangei</em>, <em>L. griseovirens</em>, and <em>L. poliochloodes</em>. <em>Lepiota grangei</em>, known from Europe, is distinguished from <em>L. brunneoolicaceae</em> based on darker and more intense green to blue-green colours, much larger basidiospores (8.0–13.0 × 3.4–4.6 µm), and septate pileus covering cells (<a href="#R110" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R110">Vellinga &amp; Huijser 1993</a>). <em>Lepiota</em> griseovirens, known from North Africa and Europe, can be distinguished from <em>L. brunneoolivacea</em> by grey-black or greyish olive-brown basidiomata with green, blue hues towards the pileus margin, slightly larger basidiospores (6.4–9.5 × 3.3–4.3 µm), and by pigments encrusted in the subpellis (<a href="#R110" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R110">Vellinga &amp; Huijser 1993</a>). The macromorphologically most similar species is <em>L. poliochloodes</em>, also described from Europe, with green grey, grey olive, grey brown, light olive brown colours; however, <em>L. poliochloodes</em> differs from <em>L. brunneoolivacea</em> by the pileus covering with short clavate cells, septa, and pigments encrusted in the subpellis (<a href="#R110" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R110">Vellinga &amp; Huijser 1993</a>, <a href="#R99" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R99">Sysouphanthong <em>et al.</em> 2011</a>).</p>
<p><strong><em>Lepiota flavonigrescens</em></strong> Sarawi &amp; Reschke, <strong><em>sp. nov.</em></strong> MB 854023. <a href="#F11" class="usa-link">Fig. 11</a>.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="F11"><h5 class="obj_head">Fig. 11.</h5>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12798316_per-2025-55-9_F11.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/d006/12798316/278f5dade7b8/per-2025-55-9_F11.jpg" loading="lazy" height="967" width="682" alt="Fig. 11"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/F11/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Basidiomata and microstructures of <em>Lepiota flavonigrescens</em>. <strong>A.</strong> Basidiomata (SeSa305, holotype). <strong>B.</strong> Basidioma (SeSa306), seen from above (left) and from below the pileus (right). <strong>C, D.</strong> Dried basidiomata (C. SeSa305; D. SeSa306). <strong>E.</strong> Basidiospores (SeSa305). <strong>F.</strong> Basidia (SeSa305). <strong>G.</strong> Cheilocystidia (SeSa305). <strong>H.</strong> Pileus covering (SeSa305). Scale bar: A = 8 mm; B = 1.2 cm; C, D = 1 cm; E = 3 µm; F, G = 10 µm; H = 20 µm.</p></figcaption></figure><p><em>Etymology</em>: <em>flavus</em> (Latin) = yellow, <em>nigrescens</em> (Latin) = turning black; refers to the yellow basidiomata of this species which turn black after drying.</p>
<p><em>Typus</em>: <strong>Benin</strong>, Donga Department, Bassila, N9.001, E1.649, alt. 360 m.a.s.l., on sandy soil, in dense gallery forest with <em>Berlinia grandiflora</em>, 30 Jun. 2022, <em>S. Sarawi, R. Dramani, F. Hampe, C. Manz &amp; A. Rühl</em>, SeSa305 (<strong>holotype</strong> FR-0175181, <strong>isotype</strong> in UNIPAR). GenBank: ITS = <a href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/nuccore/PP594569" class="usa-link" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">PP594569</a>, LSU = <a href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/nuccore/PP594717" class="usa-link" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">PP594717</a>, <em>RPB2</em> = <a href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/nuccore/PP841226" class="usa-link" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">PP841226</a>.</p>
<p><em>Basidiomata</em> small to medium, staining dark brown to black when dried. <em>Pileus</em> 15–25 mm diam., convex to plano-convex or plano-concave, with umbo; at centre dark orange brown to dark brown (6C8–7, 6D8–4, 6F8–4, 7F8–6), splitting up outwards into orange brown to light brown (6C8–6, 6D8–5, 6E8–5, 7D8–6, 7E7–5) patches, on a light yellow to greyish yellow (4C5–4) background, with velar remnants at outmost margin. <em>Lamellae</em> free, moderately crowded to crowded, ventricose, 3–4 mm deep, greyish yellow (4C5–4); edge whitish; lamellulae in up to two tiers. <em>Stipe</em> 35–50 × 2–3 mm, cylindrical, at apex yellow (5A4–2), more brown (5D8–4, 6D8–4) towards base, in lower half with distinct girdles of squamules that are concolourous with pileus centre. <em>Annulus</em> not observed. <em>Odour</em> and <em>taste</em> not recorded. <em>Basidiospores</em> (80/2/2) (5.9–)6.7–7.3–7.9(–8.9) × (2.5–)3.0–3.3–3.6(–4.0) µm, Q = 1.82–2.70, Q<sub>av</sub> = 2.21–2.28, cylindrical or amygdaliform in front view and triangular in side view, with or without slight lateral knobs, with a distinct spur, hyaline, smooth, dextrinoid, non-metachromatic. <em>Basidia</em> (15–)17–20(–24) × (5.6–)6.3–7.5(–8.5) µm, narrowly clavate to clavate, with oily content, 4-spored, occasionally 2-spored, with up to 5 µm long sterigmata, basidia and sterigmata strongly incrusted with brown to dark brown necropigment dissolving in KOH. <em>Lamella edge</em> sterile. <em>Cheilocystidia</em> (14–)18–26(–31) × (3–)4–7(–9) µm, mainly subcylindrical-capitate to narrowly clavate or somewhat tibiiform, sometimes broadly clavate to almost ovoid, rarely irregularly fusiform, slightly incrusted with brown necropigment dissolving in KOH. <em>Pleurocystidia</em> absent. <em>Pileus covering</em> a trichoderm made up of (26–)42–68(–83) × (7–)11–17(–21) µm, cylindrical, erect cells or clavate to inflated cells, with rounded apex, rarely septate, strongly incrusted with brown necropigment dissolving in KOH. <em>Clamp connections</em> present in all parts.</p>
<p><em>Ecology, habitat, and distribution</em>: Basidiomata solitary or gregarious in small groups, probably saprotrophic, on soil, in gallery forests. Currently only known from Benin.</p>
<p><em>Toxicity</em>: No amatoxins and phallotoxins were detected.</p>
<p><em>Additional specimen examined</em>: <strong>Benin</strong>, Donga Department, Bassila, N9.001, E1.649, alt. 360 m.a.s.l., on sandy soil, in dense gallery forest with <em>Berlinia grandiflora</em>, 30 Jun. 2022, <em>S. Sarawi, R. Dramani, F. Hampe, C. Manz &amp; A. Rühl</em>, SeSa306.</p>
<p><em>Notes</em>: <em>Lepiota flavonigrescens</em>, section <em>Stenosporae</em>, is characterized by yellowish basidiomata with brown to orange brown squamules and yellow lamellae. Notably, these basidiomata turn black after drying. Microscopically, <em>L. flavonigrescens</em> exhibits encrusted pigmentation of the basidia, cheilocystidia, and pileus covering cells, a distinctive feature uncommon in species of the genus <em>Lepiota</em>. Encrusted pigments are known only from <em>L. nigrescentipes</em>, which differs from <em>L. flavonigrescens</em> by its white basidiomata and non-dextrinoid, ellipsoid to ovoid basidiospores (<a href="#R15" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R15">Bon &amp; Riousset 1992</a>, <a href="#R88" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R88">Salom &amp; Siquier 2009</a>). Other species that may resemble <em>L. flavonigrescens</em> in appearance include the yellowish species <em>L. baiyunensis</em>, <em>L. citrophylla</em>, <em>L. elaiophylla</em>, <em>L. luteocastanea</em>, <em>L. luteophylla</em>, <em>L. subcitrophylla</em>, and <em>L. xanthophylla</em>. However, these species can be distinguished by the absence of encrusted pigments and the lack of a black staining reaction upon drying (<a href="#R13" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R13">Berkeley &amp; Broome 1871</a>, <a href="#R40" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R40">Hongo 1956</a>, <a href="#R74" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R74">Orton 1960</a>, <a href="#R98" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R98">Sundberg 1971</a>, <a href="#R41" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R41">Horak 1980</a>, <a href="#R111" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R111">Vellinga &amp; Huijser 1997</a>, <a href="#R99" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R99">Sysouphanthong <em>et al.</em> 2011</a>, <a href="#R63" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R63">Liang <em>et al.</em> 2023</a>). Furthermore, <em>L. baiyuensis</em>, <em>L. elaiophylla</em>, <em>L. punaensis</em>, and <em>L. xanthophylla</em> can be distinguished from <em>L. flavonigrescens</em> by ellipsoid to ovoid basidiospores (<a href="#R74" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R74">Orton 1960</a>, <a href="#R111" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R111">Vellinga &amp; Huijser 1997</a>, <a href="#R95" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R95">Stallman <em>et al.</em> 2020</a>, <a href="#R63" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R63">Liang <em>et al.</em> 2023</a>).</p>
<p><strong><em>Lepiota flavostipitata</em></strong> Sarawi, <strong><em>sp. nov.</em></strong> MB 854024. <a href="#F12" class="usa-link">Fig. 12</a>.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="F12"><h5 class="obj_head">Fig. 12.</h5>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12798316_per-2025-55-9_F12.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/d006/12798316/2ceafa2da8c3/per-2025-55-9_F12.jpg" loading="lazy" height="804" width="698" alt="Fig. 12"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/F12/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Basidiomata and microstructures of <em>Lepiota flavostipitata</em>. <strong>A.</strong> Basidiomata (SeSa203, holotype). <strong>B.</strong> Basidiomata (SeSa182). <strong>C.</strong> Basidiospores (SeSa203). <strong>D.</strong> Basidia (SeSa203). <strong>E.</strong> Cheilocystidia (SeSa203). <strong>F.</strong> Pileus covering (SeSa203). Scale bar: A = 1.5 cm; B = 1 cm; C = 3 µm; D, E = 10 µm; F = 20 µm.</p></figcaption></figure><p><em>Etymology</em>: <em>flavus</em> (Latin) = yellow, <em>stipitatus</em> (Latin) = stalked; refers to the yellow stipe apex of the basidiomata.</p>
<p><em>Typus</em>: <strong>Benin</strong>, Collines Department, near Kilibo, N8.616, E2.665, alt. 320 m.a.s.l., on sandy soil, in woodland dominated by <em>Isoberlinia doka</em> and <em>Uapaca togoensis</em>, 22 Jun. 2022, <em>S. Sarawi, D. Dongnima, F. Hampe, C. Manz &amp; A. Rühl</em>, SeSa203 (<strong>holotype</strong> FR-0175179, <strong>isotype</strong> in UNIPAR). GenBank: LSU = <a href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/nuccore/PP594690" class="usa-link" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">PP594690</a>, <em>RPB2</em> = <a href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/nuccore/PP841197" class="usa-link" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">PP841197</a>.</p>
<p><em>Basidiomata</em> small to medium. <em>Pileus</em> 5–20 mm diam., first hemispherical to conical and then becoming convex to plano-convex or plano-concave, covered by slightly erect, tomentose squamules; at centre orange-brown or reddish brown to brown (7B7–8, 7C7–8, 7D7–8, 7E7–8, 8D7–8, 8E7–8), light orange, light orange red or light brown (7A5–7, 7B5–7, 7C5–7, 7D4–7) towards margin, on a white background, with velar remnants at outmost margin. <em>Lamellae</em> free, moderately crowded to crowded, ventricose, 1–4 mm deep, white to cream; edge concolourous; lamellulae in up to two tiers. <em>Stipe</em> 10–50 × 1–3 mm, cylindrical, at apex white to pale yellow (6A1–3), gradually darker, pale orange, orange-brown or reddish brown (5A2–4, 5B3–5, 6A3–5, 6B4–6, 7B3–5, 7C5–7, 7D4–8) towards base, in lower half with distinct girdles of few squamules that are concolourous with pileus centre. <em>Annulus</em> not observed. <em>Odour</em> and <em>taste</em> not recorded. <em>Basidiospores</em> (160/4/4) (5.0–)5.9–6.3–6.8(–7.5) × (2.7–)3.1–3.5–3.8(–4.4) µm, Q = 1.59–2.10, Q<sub>av</sub> = 1.77–1.89, ellipsoid in front view and subtriangular in side view, with an indistinct spur, hyaline, smooth, dextrinoid, non-metachromatic. <em>Basidia</em> (14–)16–20(–23) × (5.5–)6.9–8.2(–9.2) µm, narrowly clavate to clavate, hyaline, with oily content, 4-spored, occasionally 2-spored, with up to 3–6 µm long sterigmata. <em>Lamella edge</em> sterile. <em>Cheilocystidia</em> (15–)19–24(–26) × (5–)6–8(–9) µm, narrowly clavate to clavate or cylindrical, occasionally subutriform to narrowly utriform, hyaline. <em>Pleurocystidia</em> absent. <em>Pileus covering</em> a trichoderm made up of (47–)58–95(–116) × (8–)9–16(–23) µm, clavate or cylindrical, erect cells, with rounded apex, with pale brown to brown parietal pigment. <em>Clamp connections</em> present in all parts.</p>
<p><em>Ecology, habitat, and distribution</em>: Basidiomata solitary or gregarious, probably saprotrophic, on soil, in gallery forests and savannah. Currently only known from Benin.</p>
<p><em>Toxicity</em>: No amatoxins and phallotoxins were detected.</p>
<p><em>Additional specimens examined</em>: <strong>Benin</strong>, Collines Department, near Kilibo, N8.616, E2.665, alt. 320 m.a.s.l., on sandy soil, in woodland dominated by <em>Isoberlinia doka</em> and <em>Uapaca togoensis</em>, 21 Jun. 2022, <em>S. Sarawi, D. Dongnima, F. Hampe, C. Manz &amp; A. Rühl</em>, SeSa182; ibid., on sandy soil, in woodland dominated by <em>Isoberlinia doka</em> and <em>Uapaca togoensis</em>, 22 Jun. 2022, <em>S. Sarawi, D. Dongnima, F. Hampe, C. Manz &amp; A. Rühl</em>, SeSa215; Borgou Department, Tchatchou, Forêt de Tchatchou-Gokana, N9.026, E2.589, alt. 360 m.a.s.l., on sandy soil, in gallery forest with <em>Berlinia grandiflora</em>, 23 Jun. 2022, <em>S. Sarawi, D. Dongnima, F. Hampe, C. Manz &amp; A. Rühl</em>, SeSa243; Donga Department, near Manigri, Forêt des Monts Kouffé, N8.992, E1.823, alt. 360 m.a.s.l., on sandy soil, in woodland dominated by <em>Isoberlinia doka</em> and <em>Monotes kerstingii</em>, 1 Jul. 2022, <em>S. Sarawi, D. Dongnima, R. Dramani, F. Hampe, C. Manz &amp; A. Rühl</em>, SeSa326.</p>
<p><em>Notes</em>: Distinctive characteristics of <em>L. flavostipitata</em> are orange brown basidiomata, a yellowish stipe apex, and indistinctly spurred basidiospores. <em>Lepiota flavostipitata</em> is part of a clade that includes the (sub-)tropical species <em>L. alopochroa</em>, <em>L. brunneopileata</em>, <em>L. longisterigmata</em> and <em>L. aurantiicolor</em>. The differentiation of <em>L. flavostipitata</em> from <em>L. longisterigmata</em> and <em>L. aurantiicolor</em> is complex due to their shared habitats and the macromorphological similarity. However, <em>L. flavostipitata</em> stands out by its deeper, darker orange-brown colour and the yellowish stipe apex. Additionally, it lacks the unusually long sterigmata which are typical for <em>L. longisterigmata</em>. <em>Lepiota alopochroa</em> can be distinguished from <em>L. flavostipitata</em> by greyish orange to brownish orange colour of the stipe, by serrulate yellow to orange-white lamellae and larger basidiospores (7.5–9.3 × 3.8–4.0 µm) (<a href="#R99" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R99">Sysouphanthong <em>et al.</em> 2011</a>). The differentiation from <em>L. brunneopileata</em> is based on the stipe colour, which is consistently orange brown to brown in <em>L. brunneopileata</em>, but becomes yellowish towards the apex in <em>L. flavostipitata</em>, along with the metachromatic basidiospores of <em>L. brunneopileata</em> (<a href="#R82" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R82">Rehman <em>et al.</em> 2024</a>). Given the complexities of this species complex, molecular sequence data is recommended for accurate identification.</p>
<p><em>Lepiota pseudoignicolor</em>, known from Guadeloupe and Venezuela resembles <em>L. flavositpitata</em>, by the colours of the basidiomata and the basidiospore shape, but lacks molecular sequence data. However, <em>L. pseudoignicolor</em> differs by yellow lamellae and larger basidiospores (9–11.5 × 3–4 µm) (<a href="#R26" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R26">Dennis 1970</a>, <a href="#R77" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R77">Pegler 1983</a>).</p>
<p><strong><em>Lepiota longisterigmata</em></strong> Sarawi, <strong><em>sp. nov.</em></strong> MB 854025. <a href="#F13" class="usa-link">Fig. 13</a>.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="F13"><h5 class="obj_head">Fig. 13.</h5>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12798316_per-2025-55-9_F13.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/d006/12798316/d8adb07b010f/per-2025-55-9_F13.jpg" loading="lazy" height="852" width="700" alt="Fig. 13"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/F13/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Basidiomata and microstructures of <em>Lepiota longisterigmata</em>. <strong>A.</strong> Basidiomata (SeSa179, holotype). <strong>B.</strong> Basidiomata (SeSa112). <strong>C.</strong> Basidiospores (SeSa179). <strong>D.</strong> Basidia (SeSa179). <strong>E.</strong> Cheilocystidia (SeSa179). <strong>F.</strong> Pileus covering (SeSa179). Scale bar: A = 2 cm; B = 1 cm; C = 5 µm; D, E = 10 µm; F = 20 µm.</p></figcaption></figure><p><em>Etymology</em>: <em>longus</em> (Latin) = long or elongated, <em>sterigma</em> (Greek) = small, stalk-like structure; refers to the long sterigmata of the basidia.</p>
<p><em>Typus</em>: <strong>Benin</strong>, Collines Department, near Kilibo, N8.616, E2.665, alt. 320 m.a.s.l., on sandy soil, in woodland dominated by <em>Isoberlinia doka</em> and <em>Uapaca togoensis</em>, 21 Jun. 2022, <em>S. Sarawi, D. Dongnima, F. Hampe, C. Manz &amp; A. Rühl</em>, SeSa179 (<strong>holotype</strong> FR-0175196, <strong>isotype</strong> in UNIPAR). GenBank: ITS = <a href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/nuccore/PP594565" class="usa-link" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">PP594565</a>, LSU = <a href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/nuccore/PP594683" class="usa-link" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">PP594683</a>, <em>RPB2</em> = <a href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/nuccore/PP841190" class="usa-link" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">PP841190</a>.</p>
<p><em>Basidiomata</em> small to medium. <em>Pileus</em> 5–25 mm diam., at first hemispherical to conical, then becoming convex to plano-convex or plano-concave, covered by slightly erect, tomentose squamules; at centre orange-brown or reddishbrown to brown (7C7–8, 7D7–8, 7E7–8, 8D7–8, 8E7–8), paler, light orange, light orange-red or light brown (7A5–7, 7B5–7, 7C5–7, 7D4–7) towards margin, on a white background, with velar remnants at outmost margin. <em>Lamellae</em> free, moderately crowded to crowded, ventricose, 1–4 mm deep, white to cream; edge concolourous; lamellulae in up to three tiers. <em>Stipe</em> 10–45 × 1–3 mm, cylindrical, at apex white to cream, gradually darker, pale yellow, pale orange, orange-brown or reddish brown (5A2–4, 5B3–5, 6A3–5, 6B4–6, 7B3–5, 7C5–7, 7D4–8) towards base, in lower half with distinct girdles of few squamules that are concolourous with pileus centre. <em>Annulus</em> not observed. <em>Odour</em> and <em>taste</em> not recorded. <em>Basidiospores</em> (210/9/9) (5.3–)6.2–6.9–7.5(–10.0) × (2.9–)3.4–3.7–4.1(–4.7) µm, Q = 1.54–2.13, Q<sub>av</sub> = 1.74–1.92, ellipsoid in front view and subtriangular in side view, with an indistinct spur, hyaline, smooth, dextrinoid, non-metachromatic. <em>Basidia</em> (15–)17–21(–25) × (6.2–)7.1–8.4(–9.5) µm, narrowly clavate to clavate, hyaline, with oily content, 2- to 4-spored, occasionally 1-spored, with 5–10(–20) µm long, irregularly shaped sterigmata. <em>Lamella edge</em> sterile. <em>Cheilocystidia</em> (12–)18–27(–40) × (4–)5–7(–9) µm, narrowly clavate to clavate or cylindrical, occasionally subutriform to narrowly utriform or fusiform, hyaline. <em>Pleurocystidia</em> absent. <em>Pileus covering</em> a trichoderm made up of (28–)51–89(–118) × (6–)11–15(–19) µm, clavate or cylindrical to sometimes rather subfusiform, erect cells, with rounded apex, occasionally somewhat thickwalled, with pale brown to brown parietal pigment. <em>Clamp connections</em> present in all parts.</p>
<p><em>Ecology, habitat, and distribution</em>: Basidiomata solitary or gregarious, probably saprotrophic, on soil, in woodland and savannah. Currently only known from Benin.</p>
<p><em>Toxicity</em>: No amatoxins and phallotoxins were detected.</p>
<p><em>Additional specimens examined</em>: <strong>Benin</strong>, Borgou Department, Okpara, Forêt de Okpara, N9.237, E2.724, alt. 200 m.a.s.l., on sandy soil, in woodland dominated by <em>Isoberlinia doka</em> and <em>Uapaca togoensis</em>, 12 Jun. 2022, <em>S. Sarawi, S. Badou, D. Dongnima, F. Hampe, C. Manz, B. Olou &amp; A. Rühl</em>, SeSa112; ibid., on sandy soil, in woodland dominated by <em>Isoberlinia doka</em> and <em>Uapaca togoensis</em>, 12 Jun. 2022, <em>S. Sarawi, S. Badou, D. Dongnima, F. Hampe, C. Manz, B. Olou &amp; A. Rühl</em>, SeSa116; Collines Department, near Kilibo, N8.616, E2.665, alt. 320 m.a.s.l., on sandy soil, in woodland dominated by <em>Isoberlinia doka</em> and <em>Uapaca togoensis</em>, 21 Jun. 2022, <em>S. Sarawi, D. Dongnima, F. Hampe, C. Manz &amp; A. Rühl</em>, SeSa180; ibid., on sandy soil, in woodland dominated by <em>Isoberlinia doka</em> and <em>Uapaca togoensis</em>, 21 Jun. 2022, <em>S. Sarawi, D. Dongnima, F. Hampe, C. Manz &amp; A. Rühl</em>, SeSa185; ibid., on sandy soil, in woodland dominated by <em>Isoberlinia doka</em> and <em>Uapaca togoensis</em>, 22 Jun. 2022, <em>S. Sarawi, D. Dongnima, F. Hampe, C. Manz &amp; A. Rühl</em>, SeSa208; Borgou Department, Forêt de Wari Maro, N9.183, E2.213, alt. 310 m.a.s.l., on sandy soil, in savannah with <em>Isoberlinia doka</em>, 25 Jun. 2022, <em>S. Sarawi, D. Dongnima, F. Hampe, C. Manz &amp; A. Rühl</em>, SeSa252; ibid., on sandy soil, in savannah with <em>Isoberlinia doka</em>, 25 Jun. 2022, <em>S. Sarawi, D. Dongnima, F. Hampe, C. Manz &amp; A. Rühl</em>, SeSa253; ibid., on sandy soil, in savannah with <em>Isoberlinia doka</em>, 25 Jun. 2022, <em>S. Sarawi, D. Dongnima, F. Hampe, C. Manz &amp; A. Rühl</em>, SeSa254; Borgou Department, near Kpéssou, Forêt de l’Ouémé Supérieur, N9.261, E2.184, alt. 340 m.a.s.l., on sandy soil, in savannah with <em>Isoberlinia doka</em> and <em>I. tomentosa</em>, 28 Jun. 2022, <em>S. Sarawi, D. Dongnima, F. Hampe, C. Manz &amp; A. Rühl</em>, SeSa295.</p>
<p><em>Notes</em>: <em>Lepiota longisterigmata</em>, section <em>Stenosporae</em>, is characterized by orange brown basidiomata, indistinctively spurred basidiospores, basidia with exceptionally long irregular sterigmata, narrowly clavate, clavate to cylindrical cheilocystidia, and a trichodermal pileus covering. <em>Lepiota alopochroa</em>, <em>L. brunneoaurantia</em>, <em>L. brunneopileata</em>, <em>L. castanea</em>, <em>L. mandarina</em>, <em>L. subcastanea</em>, and species described above, <em>L. aurantiicolor</em> and <em>L. flavostipitata</em> are all macroscopically and phylogenetically close to <em>L. longisterigmata</em>. <em>Lepiota longisterigmata</em> differs from all these species particularly in its very long sterigmata, measuring 5–10(–20) µm, a characteristic that has been observed consistently in various collections from different locations. In addition to this <em>L. alopochroa</em>, known from tropical Asia, differs from <em>L. longisterigmata</em> by serrulate, pale yellow to orange-white lamellae, and relatively small basidiospores (7.5–9.3 × 3.8–4.0 µm) (<a href="#R99" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R99">Sysouphanthong <em>et al.</em> 2011</a>). <em>Lepiota brunneoaurantia</em>, known from Pakistan, differs from <em>L. longisterigmata</em> by darker and more intense colours of the basidiomata, a distinct umbo, a darker stipe, and slightly smaller basidiospores (5.2–6.2 × 2.4–2.8 µm), and significantly smaller cheilocystidia (8.9–13.1 × 4.2–5.9 µm) (<a href="#R7" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R7">Azeem <em>et al.</em> 2024</a>). Distinctive characteristics of <em>L. castanea</em> are the dark chestnut-brown colours of the basidiomata, the presence of an annulus, and the significantly larger and distinctively spurred basidiospores (7.0–14.0 × 3.0–5.5 µm) (<a href="#R81" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R81">Quélet 1881</a>, <a href="#R103" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R103">Vellinga 2001a</a>). <em>Lepiota mandarina</em>, known from China, presents red brown, orange red colours of the pileus and stipe, though it is microscopically similar to <em>L. longisterigmata</em>, except in the shape of the sterigmata (<a href="#R59" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R59">Liang 2016</a>). Macroscopically, <em>L. subcastanea</em>, known from China, differs from <em>L. longisterigmata</em> by yellowish to yellowish brown, mustard-brown, sometimes orange or reddish colours of the pileus and stipe as well as, larger distinctively spurred basidiospores (8.5–11.0 × 3.5–4.5 µm) (<a href="#R59" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R59">Liang 2016</a>). It is a challenge to distinguish <em>L. longisterigmata</em> from <em>L. aurantiicolor</em> and <em>L. flavostipitata</em>. These three species often grow together, intermixed, and are very similar macroscopically. The main distinguishing characteristic are the long sterigmata of <em>L. longisterigmata</em>. <em>Lepiota flavostipitata</em> can be distinguished by stronger orange, orange-brown colours of the basidiomata and a yellowish stipe apex. Given the complexities of this clade, molecular sequence data are needed for accurate identification.</p>
<p>Further, <em>L. longisterigmata</em> morphologically resembles <em>L. pseudoignicolor</em>, known from Guadeloupe and Venezuela, by the colours of the basidiomata and the basidiospore shape and size, but unfortunately, that species lacks molecular sequence data. However, <em>L. pseudoignicolor</em> differs by yellow lamellae and significantly larger basidiospores (9–11.5 × 3–4 µm) (<a href="#R26" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R26">Dennis 1970</a>, <a href="#R77" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R77">Pegler 1983</a>).</p>
<p><strong><em>Lepiota tyrianthina</em></strong> Sarawi, <strong><em>sp. nov.</em></strong> MB 854026. <a href="#F14" class="usa-link">Fig. 14</a>.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="F14"><h5 class="obj_head">Fig. 14.</h5>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12798316_per-2025-55-9_F14.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/d006/12798316/74425b6e3ef3/per-2025-55-9_F14.jpg" loading="lazy" height="859" width="700" alt="Fig. 14"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/F14/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Basidiomata and microstructures of <em>Lepiota tyrianthina</em>. <strong>A.</strong> Basidioma (SeSa213, holotype), seen from above (left) and from below the pileus (right). <strong>B.</strong> Basidioma (SeSa111) seen from above (left) and from below the pileus (right). <strong>C.</strong> Basidiospores (SeSa213). <strong>D.</strong> Basidia (SeSa213). <strong>E.</strong> Cheilocystidia (SeSa213). <strong>F.</strong> Pileus covering (SeSa213). Scale bar: A = 1 cm; B = 6 mm; C = 3 µm; D, E = 10 µm; F = 20 µm.</p></figcaption></figure><p><em>Etymology</em>: The epithet refers to the colour of the basidiomata, reminiscent of Tyrian purple, a deep purple hue historically obtained from certain species of mollusks in the Muricidae.</p>
<p><em>Typus</em>: <strong>Benin</strong>, Collines Department, near Kilibo, N8.616, E2.665, alt. 320 m.a.s.l., on sandy soil, in woodland dominated by <em>Isoberlinia doka</em> and <em>Uapaca togoensis</em>, 22 Jun. 2022, <em>S. Sarawi, D. Dongnima, F. Hampe, C. Manz &amp; A. Rühl</em>, SeSa213 (<strong>holotype</strong> FR-0175205, <strong>isotype</strong> in UNIPAR). GenBank: ITS = <a href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/nuccore/PP594573" class="usa-link" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">PP594573</a>, LSU = <a href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/nuccore/PP594692" class="usa-link" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">PP594692</a>, <em>RPB2</em> = <a href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/nuccore/PP841200" class="usa-link" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">PP841200</a>.</p>
<p><em>Basidiomata</em> small to medium. <em>Pileus</em> 10–15 mm diam., convex to plano-convex, with or without umbo; at centre with a dark purple to almost blackish (14F8–7) calotte; surface around calotte splitting up into magenta to violet (14C8–7, 14D8–6) patches, on a white to cream background, or not splitting up and hardly showing the background. <em>Lamellae</em> free, moderately crowded to crowded, ventricose, 2–3 mm deep, white to cream; edge concolourous; lamellulae in up to two tiers. <em>Stipe</em> 30–35 × 1–2 mm, cylindrical, curved at base, at apex cream to yellowish (3B2–1), more pinkish, magenta to purple (14C8–3, 14D8–6) towards base, in lower half with distinct girdles of squamules that are concolourous with pileus centre. <em>Odour</em> and <em>taste</em> not recorded. <em>Basidiospores</em> (80/2/2) (5.4–)6.3–7.0–7.7(–8.6) × (2.8–)3.1–3.4–3.7(–4.1) µm, Q = 1.72–2.37, Q<sub>av</sub> = 2.00–2.10, cylindrical in front view, triangular in side view, with two lateral knobs, with a distinct spur, hyaline, smooth, dextrinoid, non-metachromatic. <em>Basidia</em> (15–)17–22(–25) × (6.1–)6.5–7.7(–8.4) µm, narrowly clavate to clavate, hyaline, with oily content, 4-spored, occasionally 2-spored, with up to 6 µm long sterigmata. <em>Lamella edge</em> sterile. <em>Cheilocystidia</em> (15–)18–25(–31) × (5–)6–7(–8) µm, narrowly clavate to clavate or cylindrical, occasionally subutriform to narrowly utriform or fusiform, hyaline. <em>Pleurocystidia</em> absent. <em>Pileus covering</em> a trichoderm made up of (44–)60–105(–151) × (6–)8–13(–18) µm, cylindrical, erect cells, with rounded apex, mainly non septate, rarely with up to 2 septa, with pale brown parietal pigment and pale purple intracellular pigment; purple pigments dissolving in KOH. <em>Clamp connections</em> present in all parts.</p>
<p><em>Ecology, habitat, and distribution</em>: Basidiomata solitary, probably saprotrophic, on soil, in woodland. Currently only known from Benin.</p>
<p><em>Toxicity</em>: No amatoxins and phallotoxins were detected.</p>
<p><em>Further specimen examined</em>: <strong>Benin</strong>, Borgou Department, Okpara, Forêt de Okpara, N9.237, E2.724, alt. 200 m.a.s.l., on sandy soil, in woodland dominated by <em>Isoberlinia doka</em> and <em>Uapaca togoensis</em>, 12 Jun. 2022, <em>S. Sarawi, S. Badou, D. Dongnima, F. Hampe, C. Manz, B. Olou &amp; A. Rühl</em>, SeSa111.</p>
<p><em>Notes</em>: <em>Lepiota tyrianthina</em>, section <em>Stenosporae</em>, is characterized by dark purple, magenta to violet basidiomata, the lack of an annulus, and distinctly spurred, dextrinoid, basidiospores with lateral knobs. Phylogenetically close or morphologically similar species are <em>L. erythrostricta</em>, <em>L. ignicolor</em>, <em>L. pilodes</em>, and <em>L. subalba</em>. <em>Lepiota tyrianthina</em> differs from <em>L. ignicolor</em>, <em>L. pilodes</em>, and <em>L. subalba</em> by the purple to violet coloured basidiomata (compare with descriptions by <a href="#R17" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R17">Bresadola 1892</a>, <a href="#R74" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R74">Orton 1960</a>, <a href="#R110" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R110">Vellinga &amp; Huijser 1993</a>). <em>Lepiota erythrosticta</em>
<a href="#R13" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R13">Berk. &amp; Broome (1871)</a> was described from Sri Lanka and is the morphologically closest species to <em>L. tyrianthina</em> because of its purple to violet basidiomata and similar micro-characteristics. <a href="#R75" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R75">Pegler (1972)</a> examined the type specimen of <em>L. erythrosticta</em> and extended the description of <a href="#R13" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R13">Berkeley &amp; Broome (1871)</a>. <em>Lepiota tyrianthina</em> differs from <em>L. erythrosticta</em> by more fragile basidiomata, the absence of an annulus, and basidiospores with lateral knobs. <em>Lepiota erythrosticta</em> has been reported several times from different geographical regions, however, the descriptions differ from those of <a href="#R13" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R13">Berkeley &amp; Broome (1871)</a> and <a href="#R75" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R75">Pegler (1972)</a> in colour and basidiospore-characteristics for specimens from Sri Lanka (<a href="#R25" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R25">Dennis 1952</a>, <a href="#R99" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R99">Sysouphanthong <em>et al.</em> 2011</a>, <a href="#R59" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R59">Liang 2016</a>). None of these specimens resemble <em>L. tyrianthina</em>. Two ITS sequences of specimens from the herbarium Lille (LIP), France, deposited as <em>L.</em> cf. <em>erythrosticta</em> collected in Martinique, are close to those of <em>L. tyrianthina</em>, however, they differ by a p-distance of 14 %. <em>Lepiota erythrosticta</em> as currently treated by various authors includes several species, and further investigations are needed. Unfortunately, sequence data of the type specimen or authentic material is not available.</p></section></section><section id="s3_3"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Feature-based molecular networking</h3>
<p>The feature-based molecular network was generated from UHPLC-HRMS<sup>2</sup> data of 96 extracts belonging to 46 species of <em>Lepiota</em> and one extract from <em>Echinoderma asperum</em>. To map the chemical diversity of the 97 fungal extracts, these data were pre-processed using MZMine 3 and the resulting 4356 mass features were organized into a molecular network, consisting of 177 clusters with at least two nodes (<a href="#F15" class="usa-link">Fig. 15</a>). To visualize the distribution of the metabolites across the samples, the molecular network was mapped at different taxonomic levels (sections or subsections), using a colour tag. At first glance, the molecular network reveals that some groups of chemical compounds are restricted to specific sections of <em>Lepiota</em>, suggesting that closely related organisms produce closely related chemical compounds that may be specific to a section/subsection.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="F15"><h4 class="obj_head">Fig. 15.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12798316_per-2025-55-9_F15.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/d006/12798316/7584440affe4/per-2025-55-9_F15.jpg" loading="lazy" height="550" width="704" alt="Fig. 15"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/F15/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Feature-based molecular network generated from the UHPLC-HRMS<sup>2</sup> data of 46 <em>Lepiota</em> extracts and one <em>Echinoderma asperum</em> extract. The network is colour coded on section level. Fourteen clusters are highlighted in red boxes (C1–C14) and colour coded on species level. Parent masses are indicated at the nodes. Nodes dereplicated against GNPS spectral libraries are indicated by blue rectangles. Black circles and red numbers indicate compounds proposed by DEREPLICATOR and cited in <a href="#T2" class="usa-link">Table 2</a>.</p></figcaption></figure><p>The MS/MS data were dereplicated against the GNPS spectral libraries, resulting in 57 hits in total. These compounds mainly correspond to acylamides, diketopiperazines, phosphatidylcholines, or phosphatidylethanolamines, but also to <em>N-</em>hydroxyoxoamidines-containing alkaloids, namely birnbaumins A–B.</p></section><section id="s3_4"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Birnbaumin cluster C1</h3>
<p>Birnbaumins A–B occur in cluster C1 (<a href="#F15" class="usa-link">Fig. 15</a>) which refers to compounds from species of <em>Lepiota</em> belonging to the sections <em>Eriophorae</em>, <em>Stenosporae</em>, and <em>Helveolae</em>. Most of these species, viz., <em>L. elaiophylla</em>, <em>L. flavonigrescens</em>, <em>L. jacobi</em>, <em>Lepiota</em> sp. (SeSa255), and <em>Lepiota</em> sp. (SeSa276), develop yellow basidiomata, consistent with the colour of these pigments.</p></section><section id="s3_5"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Amatoxin cluster C2</h3>
<p>Amatoxin-like peptides are thought to be present in some of the species studied, so the variable dereplication mode of DEREPLICATOR was applied to allow the putative identification of unknown peptide natural products from known analogues. This dereplication process resulted in 28 peptide spectrum matches (PSM) (<a href="#T2" class="usa-link">Table 2</a>). Twenty-eight fungal peptide products had been proposed, including two amatoxin-type bicyclic peptides (α- and β-amanitin) (<a href="#T2" class="usa-link">Table 2</a>). Six nodes tentatively assigned to amatoxins belong to cluster C2 referring to species of section <em>Helveolae</em>. Notably, two features of this cluster were annotated as putative amatoxins by the TIMA workflow, namely α-amanitin (initial score: 0.20, final TIMA score: 0.48) and amaninamide (initial score: 0.18, final TIMA score: 0.48). In order to strengthen our confidence in these assignments, the samples had been co-chromatographed against extracts of <em>Amanita phalloides</em> and <em>A. virosa</em> for direct comparison against standards (see below, <a href="#F19" class="usa-link">Fig. 19</a>) with the same results.</p>

<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="table/T2/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div></section><figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="F19"><h4 class="obj_head">Fig. 19.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12798316_per-2025-55-9_F19.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/d006/12798316/fddd83e034b5/per-2025-55-9_F19.jpg" loading="lazy" height="762" width="700" alt="Fig. 19"></a></p>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12798316_per-2025-55-9_F19a.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/d006/12798316/80f1547058ff/per-2025-55-9_F19a.jpg" loading="lazy" height="763" width="699" alt="Fig. 19"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/F19/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Reconstructed Ion Chromatograms (RIC) for the precursor masses of amatoxins and phallotoxins in the benchmark extracts of <em>Amanita phalloides</em> and <em>A. virosa</em>, as well as selected extracts of <em>Lepiota</em> specimens investigated for amatoxins and phallotoxins. Numbers at peaks refer to compounds listed in <a href="#T2" class="usa-link">Table 2</a>.</p></figcaption></figure></section><section id="s3_6"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Cyclic-peptide-containing cluster C5</h3>
<p>Cluster C5 reflects the presence of compounds from fungi belonging to sections <em>Eriophorae</em> and <em>Stenosporae</em>. These compounds had a mass range from <em>m/z</em> 890 to <em>m/z</em> 937, somewhat similar to the mass of amatoxins. One MS/MS feature appearing in <em>L. aurantiicolor</em>, <em>L. boudieri</em>, <em>L. flavostipitata</em>, <em>L. jacobi</em>, <em>L. longisterigmata</em>, and <em>Lepiota</em> sp. (SeSa346) was annotated by DEREPLICATOR as a phomafungin analogue, a cyclic depsipeptide formerly reported from a fungal source (<em>Phoma</em> sp.).</p></section><section id="s3_7"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">
<em>Stenosporae</em> cluster C6</h3>
<p>Compounds in cluster C6 were exclusively found in species of section <em>Stenosporae</em>. For cluster C6 no hits by DEREPLICATOR were obtained would be prefered.</p></section><section id="s3_8"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">
<em>Eriophorae</em> clusters C3 and C9</h3>
<p>The clusters C3 and C9 both refer to compounds of <em>L. minutoflava</em> in section <em>Eriophorae</em>. Most nodes in cluster C3 disclose a mass-to-charge ratio of <em>m/z</em> 182.045, consistent with a molecular formula of C<sub>8</sub>H<sub>7</sub>NO<sub>4</sub>. Some other nodes were tentatively assigned to benzoxazinones, pyridine carboxylic acids, and/or benzoxazole. In cluster C9, one node was annotated by DEREPLICATOR as a destruxin analogue. Based on TIMA, some peptide compounds were tentatively proposed for nodes of this cluster with cosine scores between 0.06 and 0.08.</p></section><section id="s3_9"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Massetolide-tagged cluster C13</h3>
<p>Five nodes in cluster C13 were tentatively assigned to cyclic depsipeptides related to massetolides by DEREPLICATOR. TIMA confirmed this result for some nodes in this group, for other nodes massetolide derivatives or viscosin derivatives were proposed, with an acceptable degree of spectrometric correspondence (cosine values close to 0.15 for some of them).</p></section><section id="s3_10"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">
<em>Eriophorae</em> dominated cluster C12</h3>
<p>Most compounds clustering in C12 were found in basidiomata of <em>L. jacobi</em> (section <em>Eriophorae</em>), however, some nodes referred to sections <em>Lilacea</em>, <em>Stenosporae</em>, or section <em>Helveolae.</em> These compounds apparently are closely related to each other, as their masses span a narrow mass range from <em>m/z</em> 583.24 to <em>m/z</em> 603.2546, including various isomeric forms. For cluster C12, DEREPLICATOR could not lead to any annotation. The MS/MS features in this cluster did not lead to good spectrometric matches against TIMA (initial score constantly below 0.03), precluding any attempt to assign a chemical class to these compounds. Many of these results reveal a spectral similarity score of zero.</p></section><section id="s3_11"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">
<em>Lilaceae</em> clusters C4, C7, C8, C10, and C11</h3>
<p>Clusters C4, C7, C8, C10, and C11 were detected for species of section <em>Lilaceae</em>; however, cluster C11 corresponds to sections <em>Lilaceae</em> and <em>Stenosporae</em>. At species level, cluster C4 was exclusively assigned to <em>L. psalion</em>. Clusters C7 and C8 were assigned to <em>L. albogranulosa</em>. The compounds found in these two groups have the same mass range (with <em>m/z</em> ranging from 615 to 653) and disclose similar TIMA-based annotation trends. The spectrometric-based annotation of these clusters provides cosine scores constantly below 0.05 (<a href="#F17" class="usa-link">Fig. 17</a>). Nevertheless, consistent with their hydrophobic nature, nodes belonging to this group have been repeatedly annotated as terpene compounds, and plant-derived limonoids or diterpene alkaloids, such as aconitane. The annotations within these clusters resulted in better TIMA final scores, sometimes reaching values up to 0.3, however with low spectrometric support.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="F17"><h4 class="obj_head">Fig. 17.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12798316_per-2025-55-9_F17.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/d006/12798316/06d54e7861c1/per-2025-55-9_F17.jpg" loading="lazy" height="725" width="696" alt="Fig. 17"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/F17/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Feature-based molecular network generated from the UHPLC-HRMS<sup>2</sup> data of 46 <em>Lepiota</em> extracts and one <em>Echinoderma asperum</em> extract. TIMA highest initial scores are indicated for each node by a colour gradient. White nodes refer to compounds not annotated by TIMA.</p></figcaption></figure><p>Cluster C10 was exclusively assigned to <em>L. cristata</em>. The nodes appearing in this cluster are related to highly hydrophobic compounds which could not be satisfyingly annotated by TIMA but a possible glycosphingolipid or a steroidic nature has been suggested by this tool, with low confidence.</p>
<p>Cluster C11 was constrained to <em>L. ochraceofulva</em>, and one node was additionally assigned to <em>L. brunneoolivacea</em>. Compared to C10, the C11 cluster appears to benefit from a slightly better TIMA annotation, especially when considering the final score. The DEREPLICATOR workflow did not provide any hit for any node of the clusters C4, C7, C8, C10, and C11.</p></section><section id="s3_12"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">
<em>Helveolae</em> and <em>Stenosporae</em> cluster C14</h3>
<p>Cluster C14 corresponds to compounds of basidiomata of <em>Lepiota</em> species belonging to section <em>Helveolae</em> and section <em>Stenosporae</em>. It presents at least two subclusters, which mainly stem from yellow species <em>L. elaiophylla</em> and <em>Lepiota</em> sp. (SeSa276) or from <em>L. rubella</em> and <em>L. subincarnata</em>. No annotation had been proposed by DEREPLICATOR. TIMA-based annotations for this group sometimes suggested the presence of Basidiomycota-derived compounds (e.g. terphenyl quinones, naphthoquinone-type dimers, etc.) with final scores of 0.4 to 0.5 for some nodes. However, these annotations rely predominantly on biological score and MS1derived annotation and lack convincing MS/MS spectral similarity to <em>in silico</em> predicted spectra, resulting in low overall confidence.</p></section><section id="s3_13"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Amatoxins in <em>Lepiota</em> species</h3>
<p>In order to increase confidence in the annotations of amatoxins and phallotoxins in <em>Lepiota</em> spp. resulting from the DEREPLICATOR-based annotation and to identify amatoxin analogues that had not been annotated, all extracts were screened for the occurrence of amatoxins and phallotoxins by UHPLC-HRMS<sup>2</sup>. Extracts of the benchmark species <em>Amanita phalloides</em> and <em>A. virosa</em> were used to compare the retention times in our chromatographic conditions and MS/MS fragmentation patterns (<a href="#F19" class="usa-link">Fig. 19</a>).</p>
<p>Reconstructed Ion Chromatograms (RIC) generated for the amatoxins and phallotoxins precursor masses in these two <em>Amanita</em> species provided reference retention time that supported the occurrence of α-amanitin and amaninamide in <em>L. brunneoincarnata</em>, of α-amanitin and β-amanitin in <em>L. elaiophylla</em> and <em>Lepiota</em> sp. (SeSa276), and of α-amanitin and γ-amanitin in <em>L. subincarnata</em>. The distinction of the isomeric γ-amanitin and amaninamide is further supported by the inspection of their MS<sup>2</sup> spectra that revealed diagnostic <em>m/z</em> ions at 243.13 and 357.18 for the former and at <em>m/z</em> 259.13 and 373.15 (<a href="#F19" class="usa-link">Fig. 19</a>, <a href="#T3" class="usa-link">Table 3</a>; <a href="#R89" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R89">Sarawi <em>et al.</em> 2022</a>, <a href="#R123" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R123">Zhao <em>et al.</em> 2023</a>).</p>
<section class="tw xbox font-sm" id="T3"><h4 class="obj_head">Table 3.</h4>
