<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="table/T3/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div></section><p>To confirm the absence of amatoxins and phallotoxins as determined by the FBMN, the presence of amatoxins and phallotoxins in all the investigated <em>Lepiota</em> species was studied at MS1 level for the masses of all amatoxins and phallotoxins. No amatoxins or phallotoxins were detected in <em>L. apatelia</em>, <em>L. albogranulosa</em>, <em>L. aurantiicolor</em>, <em>L. beninensis</em>, <em>L. boudieri</em>, <em>L. brunneoolivacea</em>, <em>L. castanea</em>, <em>L. clypeolaria</em>, <em>L. cristata</em>, <em>L. echinobispora</em>, <em>L. erminea</em>, <em>L. farinolens</em>, <em>L. felina</em>, <em>L. flavonigrescens</em>, <em>L. flavostipitata</em>, <em>L. fuscovinacea</em>, <em>L. griseovirens</em>, <em>L. ignivolvata</em>, <em>L. jacobi</em>, <em>L. lilacea</em>, <em>L. lilaceostriata</em>, <em>L. longisterigmata</em>, <em>L. magnispora</em>, <em>L. minutisterigmata</em>, <em>L. minutoflava</em>, <em>L. ochraceofulva</em>, <em>L. oreadiformis</em>, <em>L. psalion</em>, <em>L. pseudovenenosa</em>, <em>L. rubella</em>, <em>Lepiota</em> sp. (SeSa255), <em>Lepiota</em> sp. (SeSa261), <em>Lepiota</em> sp. (SeSa262), <em>Lepiota</em> sp. (SeSa278), <em>Lepiota</em> sp. (SeSa307), <em>Lepiota</em> sp. (SeSa308), <em>Lepiota</em> sp. (SeSa318), <em>Lepiota</em> sp. (SeSa325), <em>Lepiota</em> sp. (SeSa335), <em>Lepiota</em> sp. (SeSa336), <em>Lepiota</em> sp. (SeSa346), <em>L. subalba</em>, <em>L. tomentella</em>, <em>L. tyrianthina</em>, and <em>L. woehnertii</em>. Additionally, no amatoxins or phallotoxins were detected in <em>Echinoderma asperum</em>.</p></section></section><section id="s4"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">DISCUSSION</h2>
<p>In this study, 13 new <em>Lepiota</em> species were described as new to science whereas only one previously described species, <em>L. albogranulosa</em>, was found among our 104 collections from Benin made in 2022. This demonstrates the large potential for <em>Lepiota</em> species yet to be discovered in West Africa, simultaneously highlighting a significant gap in our current understanding of <em>Lepiota</em> diversity in the region. Moreover, the herein generated sequence data yielded the first multilocus tree for <em>Lepiota</em> species including numerous sequences from West Africa with 353 newly generated sequences from three different gene loci (ITS, LSU, and <em>RPB2</em>).</p>
<section id="s4_1"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Occurrence of amatoxins within section <em>Helveolae</em>
</h3>
<p>A recent UHPLC-HRMS<sup>2</sup> study demonstrated that there are fewer amatoxin-containing <em>Lepiota</em> species than previously assumed and that these are restricted to section <em>Helveolae</em> (<a href="#R89" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R89">Sarawi <em>et al.</em> 2022</a>). These results are confirmed in the present study. However, due to a larger sampling and examination of more species, chemotaxonomic relationships can now be defined more precisely. Amatoxins apparently are only present in section <em>Helveolae</em> but are absent within two subclades, one around <em>L. rubella</em> and one around <em>L. pseudovenenosa</em> (<a href="#F1" class="usa-link">Fig. 1</a>). This hypothesis must be investigated by further UHPLC-HRMS<sup>2</sup> or other methods to detect amatoxins and by phylogenetic analyses, as not all the species in this subsection have been analysed for their amanitin content. As long as such data are not available, the three species placed within the amatoxin containing clade, <em>L. brunneosquamulosa</em>, <em>L. baiyunensis</em>, and <em>L. punaensis</em>, but also <em>L. pleurocystidiata</em>, which is placed in between the amatoxin containing clade and one of the two other clades, should be suspected to contain amanitin.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="F1"><h4 class="obj_head">Fig. 1.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12798316_per-2025-55-9_F1.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/d006/12798316/f06f3f0daf35/per-2025-55-9_F1.jpg" loading="lazy" height="984" width="699" alt="Fig. 1"></a></p>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12798316_per-2025-55-9_F1a.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/d006/12798316/31521e3bc617/per-2025-55-9_F1a.jpg" loading="lazy" height="645" width="699" alt="Fig. 1"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/F1/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Maximum-Likelihood phylogram of <em>Lepiota</em> species and the outgroup based on concatenated alignments of ITS, LSU, and <em>RPB2</em>. Maximum-Likelihood bootstrap values (&gt; 70 %) and posterior probabilities obtained from Bayesian analysis (0.90) are shown on the upper side of each branch and transfer bootstrap expectations (&gt; 70 %) are shown on the lower side. The geographic origin is indicated by two-letter codes according to ISO 3166. Sequences of <em>Lepiota</em> specimens from Benin are highlighted in <strong>bold</strong>. Information about amatoxin occurrence of <em>L. venenata</em> and <em>L. subvenenata</em> obtained from <a href="#R20" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R20">Cai <em>et al.</em> (2018)</a> and <a href="#R122" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R122">Zhang <em>et al.</em> (2019)</a>. Scale bar = estimated changes per nucleotide.</p></figcaption></figure></section><section id="s4_2"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">First amatoxin-containing <em>Lepiota</em> species from Benin</h3>
<p>The first amatoxin containing <em>Lepiota</em> specimen (SeSa276) from West Africa is reported here. It is closely related to <em>L. elaiophylla</em> and contains α-amanitin and β-amanitin based on UHPLC-HRMS<sup>2</sup> results, a DEREPLICATOR-annotated feature-based molecular network (FBMN), and comparison against extracts of <em>Amanita phalloides</em> and <em>A. virosa</em> which revealed similar retention times and identical fragmentation patterns. There were no amatoxin containing <em>Lepiota</em> species known from West Africa before this study. This is the only investigated <em>Lepiota</em> species in Benin containing amatoxins. However, there still is a high risk of fatal poisoning due to the high number of unknown <em>Lepiota</em> species in West Africa and the incomplete knowledge about the occurrence of amatoxins.</p></section><section id="s4_2a"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Chemotaxonomy of <em>Lepiota</em> species</h3>
<p>Through FBMN, chemotaxonomical information is provided for <em>Lepiota</em> by some clusters of compounds being restricted to certain phylogenetic groups.</p></section><section id="s4_3"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Amatoxins as a chemotaxonomic character</h3>
<p>The amatoxin cluster C2 of the FBMN confirmed that amatoxins occur exclusively in section <em>Helveolae</em> but not in all species of this group. <em>Lepiota</em> species, however, are not referenced as amatoxin-producers in LOTUS resulting in a low TIMA biological score of 0.6 for α-amanitin and of 0 for amaninamide (<a href="#F16" class="usa-link">Fig. 16</a>). The occurrence of amatoxins is supported by the TIMA annotation based on the initial score and final score for α-amanitin (initial score: 0.20, final TIMA score: 0.48) and amaninamide (initial score: 0.18, final TIMA score: 0.48). The exclusivity of the amatoxins in section <em>Helveolae</em> is further supported by the targeted search for masses of all known amatoxins (viz. Reconstructed Ion Chromatogram generation) of all other <em>Lepiota</em> extracts revealing the absence of amatoxins and phallotoxins outside section <em>Helveolae</em>.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="F16"><h4 class="obj_head">Fig. 16.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12798316_per-2025-55-9_F16.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/d006/12798316/5ca70914803f/per-2025-55-9_F16.jpg" loading="lazy" height="718" width="689" alt="Fig. 16"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/F16/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Feature-based molecular network generated from the UHPLC-HRMS<sup>2</sup> data of 46 <em>Lepiota</em> extracts and one <em>Echinoderma asperum</em> extract. TIMA highest biological scores are indicated for each node by a colour gradient. White nodes refer to compounds not annotated by TIMA.</p></figcaption></figure><p><a href="#R89" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R89">Sarawi <em>et al.</em> (2022)</a> recently demonstrated the presence of natural compounds with masses similar to those of amatoxins in <em>L</em>. cf. <em>boudieri</em> of section <em>Stenosporae</em>. In addition, previous studies have discussed the toxicity of <em>L. boudieri</em> and other <em>Lepiota</em> species of section <em>Stenosporae</em>, based on thin-layer chromatographic results (<a href="#R32" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R32">Gérault &amp; Girre 1975</a>, <a href="#R28" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R28">Enjalbert <em>et al.</em> 2002</a>). It seems that these compounds occur in cluster C5, representing compounds from species in sections <em>Eriophorae</em> and <em>Stenosporae</em>, including <em>L. aurantiicolor</em>, <em>L. boudieri</em>, <em>L. flavostipitata</em>, <em>L. longisterigmata</em>, and <em>Lepiota</em> sp. (SeSa346). As previously indicated, the precursor masses within the cluster are reminiscent to those of amatoxins as they range between <em>m/z</em> 890 and <em>m/z</em> 937, although they belong to a distinct structural class as they clustered apart from these toxins and had not been dereplicated as such by DEREPLICATOR and TIMA. Notably, one of the nodes appearing in cluster C5 was tentatively annotated through the variable mode of the DEREPLICATOR workflow as an analogue of phomafungin, a cyclic lipodepsipeptide isolated from <em>Phoma</em> sp. (<a href="#R39" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R39">Herath <em>et al.</em> 2009</a>). The TIMA annotation, however, resulted in poorly convincing annotations, based on both spectrometric and taxonomical scoring systems.</p></section><section id="s4_4"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Putative chemotaxonomic characteristics of the <em>L. longisterigmata</em> clade</h3>
<p><em>Stenosporae</em> cluster C6 revealed nodes that are restricted to species in section <em>Stenosporae</em>, some of them being morphologically difficult to distinguish. Chemotaxonomic characteristics may be used to distinguish similar species from each other.</p></section><section id="s4_5"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Birnbaumins as a chemotaxonomic character</h3>
<p>Cluster C1 mainly includes species with yellow-coloured basidiomata. The molecular network resulted in annotating some of these nodes against the GNPS spectral libraries as birnbaumins A–B, with excellent confidence (cosine score of 0.97 for both). Birnbaumins are structurally intriguing indole derivatives reported as yellow pigments from <em>Leucocoprinus birnbaumii</em> (<a href="#R8" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R8">Bartsch <em>et al.</em> 2005</a>). The current molecular networks indicate the presence of birnbaumins A–B in the extracts of <em>L. elaiophylla</em>, <em>L. flavonigrescens</em>, <em>Lepiota</em> sp. (SeSa255), and <em>Lepiota</em> sp. (SeSa276), but not in the extracts of <em>L. jacobi</em>. However, based on the FBMN results, the presence of birnbaumin-like structures in <em>L. jacobi</em> seems possible as nodes associated with this species are found in the same cluster. Apparently, birnbaumins are responsible for the yellow colour of some <em>Lepiota</em> species. Moreover, it appears that in addition to these compounds, many unknown derivatives of birnbaumins are present in <em>Lepiota</em> species.</p></section><section id="s4_6"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Chemotaxonomic approaches to distinguish <em>L. rubella</em> and <em>L. bettinae</em>
</h3>
<p>Cluster C14 comprises nodes that are restricted to section <em>Helveolae</em> and some nodes referring to section <em>Stenosporae</em>. This cluster is split into two subclusters, corresponding to <em>L. elaiophylla</em> and <em>Lepiota</em> sp. (SeSa276) and to <em>L. rubella</em> and <em>L. subincarnata</em>.</p>
<p>The systematic position of <em>L. rubella</em> is controversial, since <a href="#R27" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R27">Dörfelt (1982)</a> described a morphologically similar species by a different name, <em>L. bettinae</em>, and <a href="#R14" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R14">Bon (1993)</a> assigned it to the genus <em>Echinoderma</em>. However, <a href="#R109" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R109">Vellinga <em>et al.</em> (1998)</a> demonstrated that <em>L. bettinae</em> and <em>L. rubella</em> are synonyms and questioned the assignment of this species to the genus <em>Echinoderma</em> by <a href="#R14" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R14">Bon (1993)</a>. Based on morphological characteristics and the multilocus phylogeny presented in the context of the current study, the position of <em>L. rubella</em> is evidently within section <em>Helveolae</em> and not in <em>Echinoderma</em>. However, it was not possible to obtain sequence data from the type specimen of <em>L. bettinae</em> conserved in HAL and it was not possible to obtain material of <em>L. rubella</em> conserved in S. Further chemotaxonomic investigations of authentic material of <em>L. bettinae</em> and <em>L. rubella</em> could help, in addition to sequence data, to support the synonymy of <em>L. bettinae</em> and <em>L. rubella</em>.</p>
<p>The second subcluster of cluster C14 is mainly restricted to <em>L. elaiophylla</em> and <em>Lepiota</em> sp. (SeSa276). <em>Lepiota elaiophylla</em> and <em>Lepiota</em> sp. (SeSa276) belong to a group of species with yellow basidiomata within section <em>Helveolae</em>. However, despite of good TIMA final scores of 0.4 to 0.5 in both subclusters, the corresponding annotations are hardly convincing. It should be noted that the few nodes with a correct degree of spectrometric match refer to sesquiterpene lactones in this group, but these have no chemotaxonomic support, and the good overall score is related to multiple nodes being tagged consistently throughout the cluster.</p></section><section id="s4_7"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Chemotaxonomy of section <em>Eriophorae</em>
</h3>
<p>Some further clusters were restricted to section <em>Eriophorae</em> but had no link with the genus <em>Echinoderma</em>. However, <em>E. asperum</em> was the only representative of the genus in our study. To obtain accurate conclusions about the chemotaxonomic differences between <em>Echinoderma</em> and section <em>Eriophorae</em>, a larger sampling size is required.</p>
<p>Two of these clusters, C3 and C9, were dominated by <em>L. minutoflava</em> with a large number of exclusive nodes. Phylogenetically and morphologically, <em>L. omninoflava</em> is most closely related to <em>L. minutoflava</em>, however, no chemical investigations were performed for <em>L. omninoflava</em> (<a href="#R43" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R43">Hou &amp; Ge 2020</a>). In this context, the cluster C9 may be of chemotaxonomic interest. The occurrence of a DEREPLICATOR-tagged node in this cluster, dereplicated as a destruxin analogue indicates the likely peptide constitution of the compounds. TIMA annotated some nodes of this cluster as diverse peptides with low confidence. To investigate the chemotaxonomic differences of <em>L. minutoflava</em> and <em>L. omninoflava</em>, further investigations of <em>L. omninoflava</em> are necessary. Thus, phylogenetically and morphologically similar species described in the future, may be distinguished based on chemotaxonomic results in addition to morphology and sequence data.</p>
<p>Cluster C12 is also mainly restricted to section <em>Eriophorae</em> and dominated by compounds obtained from <em>L. jacobi</em>, but none of the corresponding nodes could be annotated. DEREPLICATOR did not result in a single hit, which suggests that these compounds are not peptidic in nature. The MS/MS features in this cluster did not lead to good spectrometric matches against ISDB (initial score constantly below 0.03), precluding any attempt to assign a chemical class to these compounds. Many of these results reveal a spectral similarity score of zero. Some nodes were individually assigned to various structural groups when using TIMA comprising lanostanes, limonoids, or prenaphthoquinones dehydrodimers. In this context, a provisional assignment is nothing more than a query based on MS1 (i.e. the exact mass of the precursor) that may yield a few results with some degree of chemotaxonomic similarity, but a low overall confidence. This could pave the way for subsequent mycochemical investigations geared towards the isolation of these molecules of possible chemotaxonomic interest to distinguish <em>L. jacobi</em> from the morphologically similar species <em>L. echinacea</em> and <em>L. echinobispora</em>. The limited material of <em>L. echinacea</em> available did not allow a UHPLC-HRMS<sup>2</sup> analysis here.</p>
<p>Cluster C13 appears to be mostly constricted to compounds from species of section <em>Eriophorae</em> with additions from sections <em>Lilaceae</em>, <em>Stenosporae</em>, and section <em>Helveolae</em>. It is intriguing to note that DEREPLICATOR provisionally annotated five nodes of this cluster as cyclic depsipeptides corresponding to four putative massetolides and a pseudodesmine A derivative. These five compounds are antimicrobial cyclic depsipeptides with potent surfactant properties, produced by <em>Pseudomonas</em> species (<a href="#R31" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R31">Gerard <em>et al.</em> 1997</a>, <a href="#R24" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R24">De Bruijn <em>et al.</em> 2008</a>). These tentative annotations were supported by TIMA that also proposed some nodes to correspond to cyclic depsipeptides with spectrometric scores with values near 0.15 for some of them. The taxonomic distance between the <em>Lepiota</em> species studied here and the known massetolide producers (<em>Pseudomonas</em> bacteria) prevents a high final TIMA score from being achieved, due to poor chemotaxonomic consistency. Further investigations of the respective species are thus necessary on a larger scale to analyse whether <em>Lepiota</em> species can synthesize these natural compounds, which have so far only been known from bacteria, or whether it is a matter of contamination by bacteria, considering that there are bacteria that grow in and on fungal basidiomata or in symbiosis with fungi (<a href="#R16" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R16">Bonfante <em>&amp;</em> Desirò 2017</a>).</p></section><section id="s4_8"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Chemotaxonomy of section <em>Lilaceae</em>
</h3>
<p>Some clusters were restricted to section <em>Lilaceae</em> and could help to resolve the polyphyletic position of this section divided into two clades. Four of five clusters, C4, C7, C8, and C10, are restricted to section <em>Christatae</em> and one cluster, C11, is restricted to section <em>Lilaceae</em>. The molecular network annotated using the TIMA biological score indicated poor overall relevance for the different hits. However, some entries performed slightly better, particularly those derived from fungi. Such nodes appear to have been tagged as chlorophyll derivatives reported from lichenized ascomycetes so that the biological relevance of such hits is probably lower than expected (<a href="#R120" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R120">Xu <em>et al.</em> 2017</a>).</p>
<p>Cluster C11 has a good final score, due to one feature dereplicated as a metabolite obtained from a fungus-infected plant, namely makomotin B, but with a low corresponding spectrometric score, making this attribution very tentative (<a href="#R22" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R22">Choi <em>et al.</em> 2014</a>).</p>
<p>Clusters referring to section <em>Lilaceae</em> are mainly restricted to single species and do not reflect the relationship of the two clades of section <em>Lilaceae</em>. These clusters might be used at species level as chemotaxonomic characteristics but not at section level. Hence, more species of section <em>Lilaceae</em> need to be investigated by UHPLC-HRMS<sup>2</sup> to obtain more information on possible groups of natural compounds restricted to the two clades of this section.</p></section><section id="s4_9"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Application of DEREPLICATOR and TIMA</h3>
<p>This study is one of the first to apply different in silico tools, namely DEREPLICATOR and TIMA, to the annotation of tandem mass spectrometry data from samples of <em>Basidiomycota</em>. As might be expected, the number of annotations obtained by data in GNPS spectral repositories was low, due to the low number of fungal metabolites uploaded to them. Therefore, annotation coverage has been extended using DEREPLICATOR and TIMA. It is difficult to evaluate the quality of the information retrieved by these tools, as it is hardly possible to compare these annotations with the real compounds, which would require isolating them and elucidating their structure.</p>
<p>The present study, however, reveals interesting information for some specific features. Some amatoxins were annotated using both DEREPLICATOR and TIMA workflows, with this latter reaching the commonly admitted cosine threshold of 0.2 against the MS/MS spectra comprised in the ISDB database. The final TIMA score of 0.48 for these features, while convincing, is an underestimate, as LOTUS does not list any <em>Lepiota</em> species as amatoxin producers.</p>
<p>Birnbaumins A and B could be annotated with very high confidence by comparison with the experimental MS/MS spectrum comprised in the GNPS spectral libraries. These specific features confirm the value of TIMA-derived annotations, as both were suggested as the preferred candidate with good confidence (spectrometric scores against ISDB MS/MS spectra = 0.26 and 0.24 and final TIMA scores = 0.55 and 0.54, respectively).</p>
<p>In some cases (e.g. massetolide-type cyclic depsipeptides), similar annotations have been proposed by both DEREPLICATOR and TIMA, which reinforces confidence in a given putative annotation. In other cases, hypotheses are presented about the possible nature of the compounds present in a given cluster, even if the structural accuracy is often limited.</p>
<p>Overall, these elements should encourage the use of TIMA even when annotating MS/MS data from organisms for which there is currently poor chemical information in LOTUS.</p></section></section><section id="s5"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">CONCLUSION</h2>
<p>During 22 days of fieldwork in the small West African country of Benin, an immense diversity of fungi was discovered, with 96 % of the species of <em>Lepiota</em> being new to science. For each species, an overwhelming diversity of natural compounds was observed, with most of them being still unknown to science. Tropical Africa has great potential, not only in terms of species diversity but also in terms of the diversity of natural compounds. This study only scratches the surface of this great diversity and there are still many hidden gems to be discovered. We recommend using a chemotaxonomical approach to deal with this challenge, as this is most likely to yield scientifically sound results. Therefore, taxonomy and natural compound chemistry must go hand in hand in the future.</p>
<section id="s5_1"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Preliminary key to <em>Lepiota</em> species known for Benin</h3>
